  THE CANONICAL ENCODING IS AN INTERNAL BINARY FORM, NOT A WIRE ABI. Profile 1 requires the list to be
  IMMUTABLE, VERSIONED and CACHEABLE, and a cache key is a hash of bytes - so the list has a byte form
  whether or not anything sends it anywhere. What that form is NOT: stable across releases, endian-
  defined, rights-bearing, or safe to accept from another process. It carries no capability, and
  nothing validates it as untrusted input, because its only producer and only consumer are the same
  process's own `render2d`.
  What it IS: the representation the version and the semantic hash are declared against, and the one a
  round-trip test exercises - encode, decode, replay, and get the same draw. That test is worth having
  for the same reason the hash is: a list whose encoding loses a field silently produces a cache hit
  on a drawing that is not the one recorded.

- [x] Define prepared-list compatibility in the backend-neutral contract. A `PreparedDrawList` is
  bound to the profile version/hash, backend identity/version, target format, color space, physical
  extent and scale, immutable resource identity/layout generations, glyph-cache generation and
  filter parameters. `is_compatible` checks these before replay; a mismatch returns a typed
  re-prepare requirement. Track mutable image content separately: a new video frame refreshes the
  affected upload/sampling cache without re-flattening unchanged paths. Tests change each dependency
  alone and distinguish content refresh from structural invalidation.

- [x] Provide a bounded reusable `DrawList` builder that retains its capacity. Re-recording changes
  to transform, opacity, color, scroll offset or image frame within its reserved command/resource
  bounds allocates nothing; exceeding capacity returns the typed limit/reservation outcome before
  replay. Only one immutable snapshot is mutated/replaced at a time, and any live prepared snapshot
  retains its own resources. This is an in-process recording API, not a retained UI framework or a
  new cross-process transport. Re-preparation reuses reserved scratch when sufficient and explicitly
  reports a larger requirement when the changed geometry needs it.

- [x] Paths carry OPERATIONS and QUERIES, not only construction. Union, intersection, difference,
  xor and simplify; fill and stroke hit testing; tight bounds and stroke bounds; length; point and
  tangent at a distance. A vector editor, a map, a chart, a diagram and every custom control that
  responds to a click needs these, and an application that cannot ask a path whether it contains a
  point implements its own geometry - which is the private-rasteriser failure one level up.

  Boolean operations need their edge cases decided or two backends produce different unions: how
  self-intersections are resolved, what an OPEN subpath means as an operand, how two operands with
  different fill rules combine, whether the result preserves curves or is polygonised, the tolerance
  used, the canonical ordering and winding of the output contours, what happens to degenerate and
  zero-length edges, and that the whole operation is deterministic for a given input.

- [x] Flattening tolerance is ONE number shared by everything that consults geometry, and projective
  transforms give it a singularity to answer for. Curves are flattened ADAPTIVELY IN DEVICE SPACE
  with the tolerance stated in PHYSICAL PIXELS and a bounded maximum subdivision depth, and reaching
  that depth has a stated outcome rather than an implementation's guess. Under a projective
  transform, a control point at or near `w == 0` is on the projective horizon: define the epsilon,
  and define whether such a segment is clipped or the primitive refused - a curve whose control point
  projects to infinity has no useful flattening. The same tolerance governs fills, strokes, boolean
  operations, path length and hit testing; if a bounds query flattens differently from the
  rasteriser, an application asks where something is and draws it somewhere else.

- [x] Antialiasing is REQUIRED with a stated contract rather than a stated algorithm: coverage in
  `[0, 255]`, composited premultiplied, deterministic clipping, bounded scratch memory, applied to
  path fills, strokes, ellipses, rounded rectangles, transformed edges and glyphs. The backend picks
  scanline coverage or another method; the public layer fixes that an edge HAS a coverage value and
  that two backends agree on the geometry producing it. Aliased one-pixel drawing stays available as
  an explicit fast path, not as the only thing there is.

- [x] Compositing is the full set. Porter-Duff: `Clear`, `Source`, `Destination`, `SourceOver`,
  `DestinationOver`, `SourceIn`, `DestinationIn`, `SourceOut`, `DestinationOut`, `SourceAtop`,
  `DestinationAtop`, `Xor`, `Plus`. Separable blend modes: `Multiply`, `Screen`, `Overlay`, `Darken`,
  `Lighten`, `ColorDodge`, `ColorBurn`, `HardLight`, `SoftLight`, `Difference`, `Exclusion`.
  Non-separable: `Hue`, `Saturation`, `Color`, `Luminosity`. That is twelve Porter-Duff operators
  plus additive `Plus`, which is not one of them - the count and the list have to agree. The
  non-separable four are NOT deferred: they are what a theme tint, a highlight and a duotone are,
  they need the whole pixel rather than a per-channel function, and adding them later changes the
  shape of the compositing loop.

  Every mode carries its EQUATION and not only its name, because `SoftLight`, `ColorDodge` and
  `ColorBurn` have several implementations in the wild that disagree at the ends of the range, and
  the non-separable four need their luminance and saturation model and their gamut-clipping rule
  written down or they are four different pictures. State also the working space each is evaluated
  in and what happens to an out-of-range result.

- [x] Layers and group opacity: `begin_layer(bounds, opacity, blend_mode, filter)` / `end_layer`.
  This is basic UI semantics rather than an effect - two overlapping children inside a parent at
  50 % opacity must be composited together and blended once, not blended individually against the
  background. A design without layers cannot add them later without changing every drawing call that
  has an opacity.

- [x] Filters are a bounded `FilterGraph` rather than three named effects, because a filter chain over
  a layer changes the shape of a display list and retrofitting one is a rewrite of the model. Nodes:
  `GaussianBlur`, `DropShadow`, `ColorMatrix`, `Composite`, `Blend`, `Convolution`,
  `MorphologyDilate`, `MorphologyErode`, `DisplacementMap`, `Crop`, `Tile`. A BACKDROP filter has a
  place in the command model - background blur is what every current shell does behind a panel - and
  the graph is depth- and cost-bounded so a hostile document cannot ask for an unbounded chain.

  Every node declares its BOUNDS ALGEBRA, and a backdrop filter is why this is not bookkeeping: an
  input-bounds transform, an output-bounds expansion, a dependency-bounds function and a maximum
  kernel radius and cost. A blurred panel does not change only when its own content changes - it
  changes when the content UNDER it changes, because that is what it samples. Without dependency
  bounds an application redraws the panel, reports its rectangle as damage, and leaves a stale blur
  of yesterday's background behind it; with them, damage under the panel expands through the kernel
  and out to the panel's own bounds. Damage computation over a `DrawList` consults this algebra
  rather than assuming a node touches only what it covers.

- [x] Text enters through `draw_glyph_run(glyph_run, paint, transform)`, where a `GlyphRun` is
  already-shaped, already-positioned glyphs. Shaping, OpenType parsing, fallback, BiDi, line breaking
  and layout are `P02M0136`. But `render2d` must render EVERYTHING that stack can produce, not only
  the easy case: outline glyphs, grayscale masks, LCD subpixel masks where a surface supports them,
  bitmap strikes, colour-layer glyphs, embedded colour bitmaps (emoji), transformed glyphs, and
  fractional subpixel positioning. A grayscale-outline-only glyph run is not a text rendering layer,
  it is the first quarter of one.

  Colour glyphs mean the specific formats and not a category: `COLR` v0 layer lists, and `COLR` v1's
  paint graph with solids, gradients, transforms, clips and composites - which is why it is specified
  HERE, since a v1 paint graph is drawn with the very paints, clips and composites this profile
  already has. Bitmap strikes and embedded colour bitmaps come with it. SVG glyphs stay out, because
  SVG parsing is deferred by name.

  LCD subpixel masks need something from the surface or they cannot be implemented at all, so the
  surface carries `SubpixelLayout::{None, RgbHorizontal, BgrHorizontal, RgbVertical, BgrVertical}`
  and the rules that follow: an unknown layout means grayscale; a rotated or non-axis-aligned
  transform means grayscale; rendering into a transparent offscreen layer means grayscale, because
  three coverage values against an unknown backdrop cannot be composited correctly later; and the
  gamma the masks are blended in is stated. Every one of those falls back rather than refusing - a
  mandatory profile entry that a surface cannot satisfy must degrade, not fail.

  This milestone proves the RASTERISATION half end to end and stops there: the fixtures are
  pre-decoded glyph outlines, a bitmap strike and a colour glyph, and what is demonstrated is that a
  positioned `GlyphRun` of each kind renders and caches. It does NOT load a font file. Requiring that
  here would create a cycle - `P02M0136` parses fonts and depends on this part for drawing, so this
  part depending on font parsing means each waits for the other, or a throwaway parser gets written
  twice and the second one inherits the first one's bugs. Real font file to shaped run to pixels is
  proven once, in `P02M0136`, against the renderer this part delivers.

- [x] The public error type is typed and distinguishes what the caller can act on: an out-of-range
  coordinate is CLIPPED and is not an error, while an unusable target, an unsupported paint or format
  OUTSIDE the profile, a non-finite transform, an unrepresentable size and an exhausted budget are
  each their own variant. Recording into a `DrawList` validates eagerly, so an application learns at
  the call that produced the mistake rather than at replay.

- [x] Host tests for this part run WITHOUT a backend: recording, validation, transform algebra
  including projective composition and inversion, path construction, flattening tolerance, boolean
  operations against known results, hit testing, bounds, clip stack nesting, layer nesting, state
  save/restore balance, filter-graph bounds, display-list CANONICAL-ENCODING round-trip - which is
  not a wire ABI, see the `DrawList` item - and damage
  computation. Property and fuzz tests feed hostile transforms, unbounded paths, degenerate curves
  and deep nesting under strict allocation limits.

## P02M0103c - soft2d, the complete CPU implementation of Profile 1

The software backend. `no_std` core, no DisplayService dependency, draws into an `ImageViewMut` or an
`OwnedImage`. It implements the WHOLE profile; a command in Profile 1 that it cannot execute is a
defect, not an `Unsupported`.

**THE BACKEND IS IMPLEMENTED AND THE PERFORMANCE FLOOR IS NOT MET** (2026-09-12). Everything the part
asks for exists and is tested without a backend of its own: the two phases, the tiled coverage
rasteriser under both fill rules, stroking by conversion to a fill with the aliased integer line kept
as an explicit fast path, clipping as a mask with rectangular, rounded, nested, inverse and alpha-mask
clips, every paint including conic, image drawing at three qualities with a pyramid and a bounded
anisotropic tap, layers and group opacity, every operator and blend mode, the whole filter graph
including a backdrop node, glyphs of every kind behind the shared cache key, conservative damage,
tiling with per-tile binning and cancellation between tiles. The headless benchmark runner exists with
its four frozen scenes; what it reports is that the replay is between four and sixteen times over the
ceilings, and `docs/PERF.md` records the measurements, the reference host and what the remaining gap
is made of. The ceilings are fixed independently of the implementation and have not been moved.

The one YUV source the image scene names is not in it: a YUV image needs the MULTI-PLANE image model,
which `P02M0103a-common` still owes, and inventing a single-plane substitute would be a scene that
tests something else.

- [x] Implement the `render2d` backend interface in TWO phases, `prepare` then `render`, because
  "no steady-state allocation" and a profile containing layers, clip masks, blur, backdrop filters,
  path flattening, glyph caches and image pyramids cannot both be true without saying WHEN the
  scratch is worked out:

      let prepared = backend.prepare(&draw_list, target_desc, limits)?;
      backend.render(&prepared, target)?;          // allocates nothing

  `prepare` validates the resource table, flattens and caches paths, computes tile bins, resolves
  filter and dependency bounds, builds image pyramids, reserves layer and filter scratch, and returns
  either the exact memory requirement or a typed refusal. `render` replays. A frame that repeats with
  the same list and the same target then genuinely allocates nothing, and a frame that cannot fit
  says so before it starts drawing instead of failing halfway through a filter chain. It is one
  backend among future others and its public surface is the backend trait, not an application-facing
  drawing API. The composition crate alone selects `soft2d`; consumers use the trait. Prepared-list
  compatibility and content refresh follow `b`, including its negative invalidation tests.

- [x] Implement a coverage antialiasing rasteriser for filled paths under both fill rules, with
  bounded scratch storage, no allocation during a draw, and deterministic output for a given input.
  This is the core of the whole 2D stack: shapes, strokes, rounded rectangles, ellipses and glyph
  masks are all paths through it.

- [x] Implement stroking by converting a stroke to a fill - caps, joins, miter limit and dashing -
  rather than by a second rasterisation path with its own rules. Keep the aliased integer line as an
  explicit fast path with the rule it already had: integer Bresenham with BOTH endpoints included,
  ties at `error == 0` broken toward the smaller minor coordinate, and clipping applied before
  rasterising so a clipped line covers the same pixels as the visible part of the unclipped one.

- [x] Implement clipping as a coverage mask rather than a rectangle test, so rounded, path, nested
  and inverse clips cost the same machinery as rectangular ones and masks intersect. The
  axis-aligned rectangle case keeps a fast path.

- [x] Implement every paint: solid, linear, radial and CONIC gradients with multi-stop interpolation
  in linear light, `Clamp`/`Repeat`/`Mirror` spread, per-paint transform, and image patterns. Conic
  is in the profile, so it is implemented here rather than typed and refused.

- [x] Implement image drawing at quality, not only at correctness: nearest, bilinear and bicubic
  sampling; mipmapped or pyramid-based minification so a large downscale does not alias; and
  anisotropic sampling or a bounded multi-tap approximation for projective transforms. A general
  image renderer that only has bilinear produces shimmering thumbnails, which is the most visible
  defect a 2D library can ship with.

- [x] Implement layers, group opacity, every Porter-Duff operator, every separable and non-separable
  blend mode, and the whole `FilterGraph` over offscreen layer targets from `P02M0103a-common`. Layer and
  filter intermediates use the canonical premultiplied linear `R16G16B16A16_FLOAT` format, and the
  final conversion to the surface format applies tone mapping and dithering where the target is
  narrower.

- [x] Implement glyph rendering for every glyph kind the profile lists, with a bounded glyph cache
  and a mask cache for repeated shapes. **THE GLYPH CACHE KEY IS THE ONE `P02M0136` DEFINES, COPIED
  HERE RATHER THAN SUMMARISED** (2026-08-30). It was face, size, transform and subpixel phase, which
  omits the glyph identifier itself as well as everything that distinguishes two instances of one
  face - so a variable, colour, bitmap or replaced-face glyph collided with another and the cache
  returned stale pixels. The complete key is:

    face IDENTITY and GENERATION   content-derived, so two loads of one file are one face and a
                                     replaced file is a different one; the generation is what
                                     invalidates everything derived from a face that was replaced
    FACE INDEX                     which face within the file (added 2026-08-31). A TrueType or
                                     OpenType COLLECTION is one file, so every face in it shares the
                                     content-derived identity, and two of them can carry the same
                                     glyph index meaning different glyphs with every other field of
                                     this key equal. `P02M0136`'s resource contract already defines a
                                     face as identity PLUS index; leaving the index implicit here
                                     made the key and the contract disagree about what a face is
    glyph                          the glyph index, which was missing outright
    size                           and the transform, as before
    variation coordinates          the normalised axis coordinates of the instance
    subpixel phase                 as before
    glyph kind                     outline, grayscale or subpixel mask, bitmap strike, `COLR` layers
    strike and palette             where the kind uses them - the bitmap strike selected, and the
                                     `COLR` palette index
    RASTERISATION MODE             which for an LCD mask is the `SubpixelLayout` it was rasterised
                                     for (added 2026-08-30). This file requires LCD output to differ
                                     by RGB/BGR and horizontal/vertical, and to fall back to
                                     grayscale for an unknown layout, a rotated or non-axis-aligned
                                     transform and a transparent offscreen layer - so a key without
                                     it lets an RGB-horizontal mask and a BGR-vertical mask for one
                                     glyph share a cache entry, which is the stale-pixel case this
                                     key exists to prevent

  This key is jointly owned with `P02M0136` and is ONE normative definition: neither side may
  restate it in its own words, and a change to it is a change to both files in the same edit. The
  cache tests here and the shaping tests there exercise the same key, and each side has a negative
  case proving that two entries differing only in variation coordinates, generation, kind, strike,
  palette, rasterisation mode or FACE INDEX do not collide.

  THE KEY IS NOW ONE DEFINITION IN CODE (2026-09-12), which is stronger than two files agreeing:
  `src/user/libs/text/font-contract` carries `GlyphCacheKey` with all eleven fields, and both sides
  link it rather than each declaring a key of their own - so "neither side may restate it" is a
  property of the build rather than a rule a reviewer has to notice. The field-by-field negative case
  lives with the type and covers every field this paragraph names. What is still owed HERE is the
  cache itself: this item's glyph and mask caches, keyed on that type, with the renderer's own
  fixtures.

- [x] ONE implementation of pixel conversion, compositing and sampling, living in `graphics-core` and
  shared by `pix`, `soft2d`, `soft3d` and DisplayService's own copy and scale path; none carries a
  private second copy. This is already real: `pix::blend_over` is an integer straight-alpha
  source-over on sRGB-encoded bytes while a correct compositor needs linear light, so the shared
  primitive takes its WORKING SPACE as part of the contract and each caller states which it uses.
  Fix the stage ORDER once: decode transfer function -> convert straight to premultiplied -> sample
  and filter -> blend in the stated working space -> convert back to the target's alpha mode ->
  encode transfer function -> quantise with dithering. Filtering premultiplies BEFORE interpolating -
  interpolating straight alpha pulls the colour of fully transparent texels into the result and rings
  dark halos around transparent edges. And "shared" means CALLED.

- [x] Rectangles and gradients are exact rather than approximately agreed. `Rect` is half-open, a
  fill covers every sample point inside it, a one-pixel aliased `stroke_rect` lies INSIDE the
  rectangle rather than straddling its boundary, and a zero-width or zero-height rectangle draws
  nothing and is not an error.

- [x] Damage returned from a draw is CONSERVATIVE, not exact. Exact damage would mean comparing old
  and new pixels - a source-over with alpha zero changes nothing while covering a rectangle - and
  that cost defeats the purpose. Every draw returns the smallest inexpensive conservative raster
  bound: it contains every pixel that may have changed, is clipped to the target and clip stack, and
  is empty when no sample was reached.

- [x] Make it a REAL software backend, not a reference implementation: tile-based rendering with
  per-tile command binning, SIMD fast paths, cancellation when a surface is closed or resized
  mid-frame, and caches for paths, masks, glyphs and filter intermediates. A BOUNDED WORKER POOL IS
  NOT REQUIRED BY THIS ITEM and is not in `c`'s Done condition; it is added by the later performance
  item when the self-thread runtime exists (see below). Tiling, binning, SIMD and cancellation are
  what this item owns, and they are the work that makes a pool worth adding later.

  **AND THE WORKER POOL HAS NOTHING TO BUILD ITSELF FROM** (2026-08-30). `soft2d` and `soft3d` both
  make a bounded worker pool mandatory and both differential-test their parallel paths, and ordinary
  userspace has no way to create a second thread. `SYS_THREAD_CREATE` requires `MANAGE` on a Process
  capability, which an application does not hold over itself; the only wrapper is
  `rt::process_prepare`, the process-LAUNCH path, which always passes the one `USER_STACK_TOP`;
  demand stack growth is bound to that same single span, so a second thread cannot have a growable
  stack; Thread is not a waitable object, so there is no join; and Event's only reset is
  `#[cfg(test)]`, so there is no reusable wakeup for a pool. The three ways out of that without a
  mechanism are all forbidden here: granting each renderer client broad process-management authority
  over itself, hand-rolling unsafe stacks and polling inside the renderer, or silently falling back
  to the single-threaded path the Definition of Done rules out.
  So the SELF-THREAD RUNTIME is a prerequisite OF THE LATER PERFORMANCE ITEM and of nothing in `c`
  or `g`, and it is not owned here: narrow self-spawn authority that is not process-management
  authority, independently placed guarded stacks with their own Domain accounting, join and
  cancellation, allocator and per-thread state rules under concurrency, crash propagation, and
  deterministic shutdown - tested on all three architectures.

  MULTITHREADING IS OUT OF PROFILE 1 EVERYWHERE, NOT ONLY IN THE DONE CLAUSES (2026-08-30). A
  previous round removed it from `c`'s and `g`'s completion conditions and left it mandatory in the
  prerequisite matrix, in both software-renderer work items, in the 2D and 3D test items and in the
  aggregate 2D completion clause - so both parts remained unschedulable in every place that actually
  governs the work. Every one of those has been corrected: the matrix says NOT the runtime, the two
  work items own tiling and SIMD and not a pool, and the differential tests are conditional on a
  parallel path existing rather than requiring one. `c` and `g` are done when they run TILED, and a
  correct single-threaded tiled rasteriser is a conforming one.

  `P02M0135` needs the same mechanism for its foreign thread facade IF its derived inventory names
  thread creation; it has pinned a configuration that does not, so neither file is now blocked on an
  unnumbered runtime. Whoever numbers it owns one contract for both consumers; two private thread
  runtimes for one system is what this paragraph exists to prevent.

  With one rule that keeps it honest: there is a DETERMINISTIC SCALAR REFERENCE path, and every
  parallel or SIMD path THAT EXISTS is differential-tested against it - unconditional once one
  exists, and not a requirement that one be created. The bit-exact/tolerance split at the top
  of this file says which parts must match exactly - geometry classification, clipping topology and
  quantised geometry - and which match within tolerance. 2D COVERAGE IS TOLERANCE-BASED: this said
  "coverage and geometry classification" must match exactly, which contradicts the split it cites,
  because this file deliberately lets a 2D backend choose its coverage algorithm (corrected
  2026-08-30). The scalar reference and its parallel paths must still agree bit-exactly WITH EACH
  OTHER - they are one algorithm - and that is a different claim from a conformance tolerance
  against another backend.

- [x] Host tests cover path filling under both rules, coverage values along known edges, stroke caps
  and joins, dashing, clip nesting, rounded and inverse clips, layer opacity against manually
  composited reference values, every Porter-Duff operator and every blend mode against its algebraic
  definition, projectively transformed image drawing and minification, every spread mode,
  colour-space conversion and tone mapping, dithering, every FilterGraph node individually,
  representative composed graphs and backdrop-dependency propagation, glyph masks of
  every kind, returned damage bounds, guarded canaries around the target, and - where a parallel or
  SIMD path exists - scalar-versus-parallel agreement. Property and fuzz tests feed hostile
  coordinates, extents, pitches, transforms,
  degenerate curves and deep filter graphs and prove no panic and no out-of-bounds write.

- [ ] Meet an enforceable soft2d performance floor with a HEADLESS benchmark runner owned by `c`.
  It records bounded `DrawList` fixtures once and replays into `OwnedImage` without a Surface,
  DisplayService, `test2d-sw.lsexe` or part `d`. The same four fixtures are reused by `d` for the
  interactive proof; the live application does not become a prerequisite of this headless backend.

      UI-basic       rectangles, pre-decoded glyphs, images and clips
      UI-effects     layers, shadows and backdrop blur
      vector-stress  paths and curves at a fixed stroke width and join set
      image-stress   scaling and color conversion, including one YUV source

  Freeze each fixture's command/resource counts, extents, kernels and deterministic content before
  recording acceptance measurements, so a later simplification cannot lower the workload silently.
  Document the x86_64 reference host already used in `docs/PERF.md`, CPU model/core count, build flags,
  runner environment, clock, warmup and measured sample count. Measure prepared replay at 640x480:
  preparation/upload and live presentation are reported separately. Record median and 99th percentile.

      UI-basic, image-stress     median <= 16.7 ms
      UI-effects, vector-stress  median <= 66.7 ms

  These ceilings are fixed independently of implementation. The initial frozen budget per scene is
  the first accepted measurement or the ceiling, whichever is lower, and may only be lowered later.
  The registered benchmark gate exits non-zero if any median exceeds its frozen budget OR ceiling;
  a first implementation that misses a ceiling fails `c`, rather than defining a passing baseline.
  Any intentional ceiling or fixture change requires an explicit change to this plan and its recorded
  performance contract. Measure one fixed HiDPI scale and emulated aarch64/riscv64 results separately;
  those are reported and are not held to the 640x480 native reference-host ceiling.

  THE RUNNER EXISTS, THE FIXTURES ARE FROZEN, AND THE FLOOR IS NOT MET (measured 2026-09-12, re-measured
  2026-09-13 and unchanged). `src/tools/soft2d-bench`, run by `./bench.sh --suite soft2d`, records the
  four scenes and checks their frozen command and resource counts before the clock starts.
  `docs/PERF.md` carries the reference host, the build flags, the warmup and sample counts, and the
  measurements. What it also carries is the verdict this item demands of a first implementation that
  misses a ceiling: it FAILS, rather than defining a passing baseline, and the budgets in the runner
  stay at the ceilings rather than being frozen at what was measured.

      scene            replay median   ceiling   over by     (2026-09-13)
      UI-basic              46.4 ms     16.7 ms     2.8x        was 4.8x
      UI-effects           332.0 ms     66.7 ms     5.0x        was 5.9x
      vector-stress         97.0 ms     66.7 ms     1.5x        was 3.8x
      image-stress         377.7 ms     16.7 ms    22.6x        was 25.5x

  RE-MEASURED 2026-09-16, AFTER FOURTEEN CHANGES, each measured on its own on the same host:

      scene            replay median    ceiling   verdict        (was 2026-09-13)
      UI-basic          16.4 - 17.0 ms   16.7 ms   ON THE LINE    was 2.8x over
      UI-effects         179 - 183 ms    66.7 ms   2.7x           was 5.0x
      vector-stress     75.4 - 76.8 ms   66.7 ms   1.13x          was 1.5x
      image-stress         344.7 ms      16.7 ms   20.7x          was 22.5x

  `UI-basic` SITS ON ITS CEILING, WHICH IS NOT THE SAME AS CLEARING IT. Seven consecutive runs
  measured 16.36, 16.47, 16.70, 16.36, 16.66, 16.66 and 16.96 ms against 16.7 - five met it, two did
  not - which is why a RANGE is reported and not a median: a single median here is a coin toss. It
  was 46.1 ms when this work started, and that is the part worth having; "met" is not.
  THE RUNNER'S BUDGETS STAY AT THE CEILINGS, deliberately and by this item's own rule: a scene's
  frozen budget is its first accepted measurement OR its ceiling, whichever is LOWER, so accepting
  this one would freeze a number two runs in seven miss and every later run would be testing the
  weather.

  RE-MEASURED 2026-09-16 (later), UNCHANGED WITHIN NOISE, and the probes say something the scene
  numbers cannot:

      scene            replay median    ceiling   over by
      UI-basic             16.85 ms      16.7 ms    1.01x
      UI-effects          184.85 ms      66.7 ms    2.77x
      vector-stress        78.29 ms      66.7 ms    1.17x
      image-stress        345.03 ms      16.7 ms   20.66x

  **ONE OPAQUE FULLSCREEN RECTANGLE COSTS 14.55 ms**, which is 86 percent of UI-basic's entire
  measurement and 87 percent of its ceiling. That is the probe `one-opaque-fullscreen`: a single
  command, one solid opaque fill over 640x480, through the covered-run fast path that skips the read
  and the composite entirely. 307,200 pixels in 14.55 ms is 47 nanoseconds a pixel.

  **AND THOSE 47 NANOSECONDS WERE FOUND (2026-09-19), AND UI-BASIC NOW MEETS ITS CEILING.**

  THE NUMBER WAS NEVER A PIXEL'S DRAWING COST. Three probes were added whose purpose is to be
  subtracted from one another - the same fill as four quarter-screen rectangles, the same full-screen
  rectangle four times, and the top half of every tile - so the command count and the pixel count vary
  separately instead of being multiplied together. Four times the pixels at the same command count
  costs 4.3 ms more, which is **4.7 nanoseconds a pixel and not 47**; a command is about a
  microsecond, since `dot-per-tile` runs eighty-eight of them in nine hundredths of a millisecond.
  That left about thirteen of the 14.3 milliseconds in neither term.

  IT WAS `Tile::store`, MEASURED BY EMPTYING ITS LOOP: 14.29 ms became 1.34. **Ninety-one percent of a
  solid full-screen fill was the way out of the tile**, and the drawing - the rasteriser, the spans
  and the writes together - was 4.7 of it.

  AND WHAT IT WAS DOING IS THE DEFECT. `Tile::store` encoded ONE PIXEL AT A TIME, calling
  `encode_tabled` per pixel, which takes every decision the encode makes - the transfer, whether there
  is a table, the premultiply, the dither - once for each pixel instead of once for the row.
  `Encoder::encode_row` exists for exactly that and says so in its own comment, and `Surface::store`
  beside it has always used it; this path had the same loop written out by hand. The row form is used
  now, with the same dither phase - the target's own x and y, which is why `encode_row` takes the
  row's first column, because a tile-relative phase restarts at every tile boundary and looks like a
  seam.

      scene            before     after    ceiling
      UI-basic         16.85 ms  12.80 ms   16.7 ms   MET
      UI-effects      184.85 ms 179.11 ms   66.7 ms   over 2.68x
      vector-stress    78.29 ms  73.57 ms   66.7 ms   over 1.10x
      image-stress    345.03 ms 340.54 ms   16.7 ms   over 20.4x

  **UI-BASIC IS THE FIRST OF THE FOUR TO MEET ITS CEILING**, and vector-stress is now within ten
  percent of its own. The 112-scene 2D conformance suite passes unchanged, which is what says the
  dither and the transfer still produce the pixels they did.

  AND `Tile::load` CARRIED THE SAME LOOP, which is the other half of the round trip: it decoded a pixel
  at a time through `decode_tabled`, asking whether there is a usable table and whether the working
  space is linear once per pixel rather than once per row. The frozen four barely notice - they are
  held by other things - but the probes that actually pay the decode do:
  `one-translucent-fullscreen` 19.76 to 17.80 ms and `hundred-small-translucent` 16.62 to 15.15.

  THREE EARLIER HYPOTHESES ABOUT THIS NUMBER WERE ALL ABOUT THE DRAWING - the transfer encode inside
  the composite, a software square root, a cheaper store-back - and the drawing is a fifteenth of it.
  The reason none of them moved anything is that none of them was in `Tile::store`.

  **AND THE SAME DEFECT WAS ONE LAYER UP, IN THE SHADER.** The span loop asked `Shader::at(x, y)` for
  every pixel of every non-solid run, and `at` matches on the shader - the arm, the spread, the ramp,
  the transform's shape - once for each of them. The solid arm beside it already avoided exactly that
  and said why. `Shader::row` takes the decision once, and **vector-stress went from 73.42 to 67.61 ms
  against a 66.7 ceiling** - one percent over, from ten percent this morning, and stable there across
  three runs. UI-effects 183.51 to 174.82 and image-stress 340.22 to 333.34 came with it.

  THE ARITHMETIC IS UNCHANGED PIXEL FOR PIXEL, which is a constraint and not a note: a gradient's
  position is a linear function of `x` and could be advanced by a constant along the row whenever the
  paint's transform is affine, and that is NOT done, because accumulating a step rounds differently
  from evaluating the expression and the conformance suite compares these pixels exactly.

  AND THE LAST ONE PERCENT WAS LEFT DELIBERATELY. `spread_position` and `Ramp::at` still decide per
  pixel, and hoisting them means duplicating either the loop per spread mode or the ramp's arithmetic
  in a second place - which is what makes two implementations drift - for one percent of one scene.
  The composite path was checked for the same defect and does not have it.

  AND THE IMAGE SHADER TOOK THE SAME HOIST, plus one exact removal beside it: `footprint` mapped the
  device point through the paint's inverse transform to find `here`, which is the IDENTICAL expression
  the caller had just evaluated to find the texel - so a mipmapped pixel paid FOUR projective
  multiplies where it needs three. `image-photo-bilinear` 78.52 to 73.90 ms and `image-photo-mipmapped`
  75.97 to 71.38.

  **SO THE FOUR SCENES STAND AT:**

      scene          this morning       now    ceiling
      UI-basic           16.85 ms  12.44 ms    16.7 ms   MET
      vector-stress      78.29 ms  66.5-67.0   66.7 ms   AT THE LINE
      UI-effects        184.85 ms 176.99 ms    66.7 ms   over 2.65x
      image-stress      345.03 ms 329.75 ms    16.7 ms   over 19.7x

  **vector-stress IS AT ITS CEILING AND NOT UNDER IT, and that is said rather than rounded down.**
  Four consecutive runs gave 66.79, 66.84, 66.54 and 66.97 against 66.7 - one of the four under. This
  item's own rule about not freezing a number that two runs in seven would miss applies exactly as
  well to calling one met: at the line is a different fact from met, and it is the one that is true.

  **AND IT CROSSED THE LINE LATER THE SAME DAY (2026-09-19), ON ONE INVARIANT TEST.** The linear
  gradient's row already had the dispatch hoisted out of it - that is the change above - and what was
  still inside the loop was `length_squared <= 0.0`, asked of every pixel about a value that does not
  vary along a span or at all. It is decided once for the row now, and the pixels are unchanged: the
  same `ramp.at(1.0)` the arm already produced, decided before the loop rather than inside it.

  MEASURED BOTH WAYS, THREE RUNS EACH, because a third of a millisecond on a scene that straddles its
  ceiling is exactly the size of claim that needs it:

      form                      runs                      median   ceiling
      the test inside the loop  66.835  66.473  66.606     66.606   66.7 ms   one of three OVER
      the test hoisted out      66.157  66.320  66.250     66.250   66.7 ms   three of three MET

  CORRECTED THE SAME DAY, AND THE CORRECTION IS THE MORE USEFUL FACT. That paragraph said "so it is
  met", on three runs under the ceiling. It is NOT met: six later runs of the same binary read 67.13,
  67.09, 67.46, 67.69, 67.24 and 67.41, every one of them OVER. The difference between the two sets
  is not the gradient change - that is exact and the conformance suite proves it - it is that the
  suite gained a fifth scene in between, and THIS SCENE'S NUMBER MOVES BY MORE THAN THE CHANGE BUYS
  WHEN ANYTHING ELSE IN THE PROCESS MOVES.

  SO THE HOIST IS REAL AND THE VERDICT IS NOT. Measured back to back in one arrangement it is worth
  about 0.35 ms, which is half a percent; the scene's own sensitivity to what else ran before it is
  larger than that. `vector-stress` IS STILL AT ITS LINE, which is what this item said before and
  what a third of a millisecond was never going to change. The rule this file already states applies
  to the claim I made as well as to the number: at the line is a different fact from met, and calling
  it met on the arrangement that happened to be green is the same error as freezing a number two runs
  in seven would miss.

  WHAT THIS DOES NOT CHANGE: `UI-basic` 11.8 met is the one scene comfortably inside its ceiling.
  THE FLOOR STILL ASKS FOR FOUR AND HAS ONE.

  **AND `image-stress` IS TWO SCENES NOW (2026-09-19), ON THE PROJECT OWNER'S ANSWER.** This item put
  the question to them in as many words - whether a video scene's ceiling is 60 Hz at this content,
  or whether the scene is two scenes - and said an implementer may not quietly decide it. The answer
  was: two scenes.

  AND THE SPLIT IS WHERE THE CODE'S OWN COMMENTS ALREADY DREW IT. `image-resample` is eight large
  downscales and an upscale at each of the three qualities, over ONE source, with source space and
  target space the same - so what it measures is a pyramid and three filters and nothing else.
  `image-convert` is twelve wide-gamut tiles into an sRGB target, a full-frame wide-gamut draw and a
  full-frame YUV draw from its planes - two of its three kinds of draw cover the WHOLE FRAME, which
  is three hundred thousand pixels through a transfer function and a matrix.

      scene            median   ceiling   over
      image-resample   80.6 ms   16.7 ms   4.8x
      image-convert   127.1 ms   16.7 ms   7.6x

  AND THAT IS THE POINT OF ASKING. The single number was 11.4x and said which half was slow only by
  accident of how the two were summed: resampling is 4.8x and conversion is 7.6x, and they are not
  the same problem - one is a sampler's per-tap work and the other is a per-pixel transfer function
  over full frames. A change to either would have moved the old number and told nobody which it had
  helped.

  THE FROZEN COUNTS CAME WITH THEM - 11 commands over 1 resource, and 14 over 2, which sum to the 25
  and 3 the one scene recorded - so a fixture that drifts still fails rather than being measured.

  **AND THE CEILINGS ARE INHERITED AND NOT DECIDED, WHICH IS SAID HERE SO NOBODY READS THEM AS AN
  ANSWER.** Both halves carry 16.7 ms because the scene they came from did. The owner was asked
  whether a video scene's ceiling is 60 Hz at this content OR whether the scene is two scenes, and
  answered the second - which settles the SPLIT and leaves the first question open for the half that
  raised it. `image-convert` is the one that draws two full frames through a transfer function and a
  matrix, and whether sixty times a second is the right thing to ask of that is the same question,
  now asked of a scene that contains nothing else. `image-resample` at 16.7 is not in doubt: it is UI
  imagery at UI sizes, which is a per-frame cost.

  WHAT THIS ITEM WILL NOT DO IS PICK ONE. A ceiling chosen by the implementer to make a number look
  better is the thing this file refuses in as many words, and it refuses it just as firmly when the
  implementer is the one who split the scene.

  **AND THE OWNER ANSWERED THE REMAINING HALF (2026-09-21): "TRY TO OPTIMISE SO THAT IT WORKS, BUT
  NOT AT THE COST OF FUNCTIONALITY. IF IT CANNOT BE DONE, LEAVE IT."** Together with the answer to
  the four-lane question - "it must be universal for every platform", which refuses a per-architecture
  encode - that fixes exactly what an implementer may spend: work that changes NO PIXEL and NO
  INSTRUCTION SET.

  AND WITHIN THAT, IT CANNOT BE DONE, WHICH IS ARITHMETIC AND NOT A TUNING VERDICT. The numbers were
  already in this file and the head of it; what was missing was putting them together:

    - The per-pixel FLOOR of this backend is 33 ns. One full-screen opaque solid rectangle, one
      command, the backdrop decode skipped, is 10.125 ms over 307,200 pixels.
    - This scene covers the frame TWICE, so 2 x 307,200 x 33 ns is 20.3 ms with a sampler and a
      shader that cost NOTHING AT ALL - already over 16.7 before any of the work the scene is about.
    - AND THE DECISIVE ONE: the full-frame YUV draw ALONE, measured by itself, is 54.7 ms. That is
      3.3x the whole budget for a single command. No ordering, no culling and no skipping reaches a
      number below the cost of the one draw that must happen - and it is the LAST draw, so even
      dropping everything it covers (which would be exact, portable and legitimate) leaves 54.7.

  SO THE BRANCH THIS ITEM TAKES IS THE SECOND ONE THE OWNER NAMED. Nothing in the backend was
  changed for this scene, because every change that would move it is on the other side of a line the
  same answer drew. What stays open is not an optimisation: it is the CEILING, which is a statement
  about what this system promises at this content, and the two answers above narrow it rather than
  settle it.

  AND BOTH CHANGES WERE PROVED PIXEL-IDENTICAL ON ALL THREE PORTS, NOT ONLY WHERE THEY WERE MADE.
  The gradient hoist is in the 2D backend and the flat-`z` skip is in the 3D sampler, and each claims
  to compute the same answer less often - a claim only the conformance suite can settle. `image,slow`
  passes with 34 tests on each of x86_64, aarch64 (2330 s) and riscv64 (2463 s), and all three report
  `test2d-conformance: 112 passed, 0 failed, 0 unsupported, 0 untested` and
  `test3d-conformance: 160 passed, 0 failed, 0 unsupported, 0 untested` - the same counts as before
  either change.

  AND THE BENCHMARK THAT MEASURES THIS WAS NOT LISTED WHERE ANYBODY WOULD LOOK FOR IT. `bench.sh`
  carried `soft2d` in its suite map and not in its help or `--list` - along with `lico` - so the one
  suite this part's floor is defined by was the one the documented entry point did not mention. The
  help is built from the map now, a suite with no description is a hard error rather than a silent
  omission, and the guard is proven to refuse before it is trusted to print.

  AND THE TWO THAT ARE FAR OUT WERE PROBED RATHER THAN GUESSED AT, which is what today's evidence says
  to do: every change that worked came from a probe that separated a term, and every one that failed
  came from a hypothesis about where the time ought to be.

  **UI-effects HAS NO DOMINANT TERM.** The layer composite looks like the defect - it walks the layer
  pixel by pixel through `get`, `get`, `set` on a `&mut dyn Raster`, three virtual calls and a
  clip-stack walk each, with the opacity clamped again every time - and emptying it moves the scene
  from 177.0 to 172.8 ms. **Four milliseconds of a hundred and seventy-seven.** Emptying the BLUR
  instead gives 128.1, so the filter is forty-nine of them; the remaining hundred and twenty-eight are
  spread over forty-five commands and the tiles they touch. The layer composite would have been a
  rewrite for two percent, and it was not done.

  AND THE BLUR IS ALREADY THE CAREFUL VERSION - separable, run at a time in both passes, interior split
  from edges, tap order preserved so the answer is the same number and not a close one. What is left in
  it is the convolution itself, and the usual way to go faster computes a different picture, which is a
  question about the profile's tolerance for a blur rather than an optimisation.

  **image-stress IS A PER-TAP TRANSFER DECODE**, which three probes separate out: emptying
  `Sampler::texel` takes bicubic from 254.2 to 44.9 ms, and emptying only its DECODE takes it to
  115.2. So the decode is **139 of those 254 milliseconds** - the largest single term in the whole 2D
  suite - the fetch is another 70, and the cubic arithmetic is the remaining 45. A bicubic pixel takes
  sixteen taps and decodes sRGB to linear on every one, for texels its neighbours decode again a
  moment later.

  AND THE FIX IS ALREADY IN THE FILE FOR ONE CASE. `Pyramid` holds its levels in the canonical
  premultiplied linear float format and is built in `prepare`, so a MIPMAPPED draw of the same image
  costs 71 ms where the bicubic one costs 254. Decoding the source once for bilinear and bicubic too
  is the same move, and the value would be bit-identical: same decoder, same table, same order -
  decode each texel, then weight it.

  IT WAS A MEMORY DECISION AND NOT AN OPTIMISATION, so it was made rather than patched around.
  `wants_pyramid` exists precisely to choose which images pay for a decoded copy, level zero is
  sixteen bytes a texel, and `max_prepared_scratch_bytes` is a profile limit. Trading replay time for
  prepared memory is the right trade by this item's own rules - the ceiling is on prepared replay and
  preparation is reported separately - and WHICH images pay it is now stated as a second predicate
  beside the first: `wants_decoded` is the images this list samples `Bilinear` or `Bicubic`.

  **AND THE DECISION HAS TWO HALVES, BECAUSE A LIMIT IS NOT A SUGGESTION.** A required pyramid is one
  a `Mipmapped` draw cannot be served without; an optional decoded source only makes a draw faster.
  So the optional ones are built after the required ones and GIVEN BACK, newest first, until the
  prepared total is under the ceiling - a list that fitted before this existed still fits, and only
  a frame that does not fit WITHOUT any optional copy is refused. An optional copy that will not
  allocate is not an error either: the image samples the way it always did.

  AND AN OPTIONAL COPY IS LEVEL ZERO ALONE. A bilinear or bicubic draw reads level zero and nothing
  else, so the halvings under it were prepare time and prepared memory that no draw touched - 12.6 ms
  of UI-effects' preparation and a third of its bytes. `Pyramid` gained a level-zero constructor and
  the chain is built on top of it.

      scene          replay before   after    ceiling
      UI-basic           12.44 ms  11.81 ms   16.7 ms   MET
      vector-stress      66.5-67.0 66.5-67.0  66.7 ms   AT THE LINE
      UI-effects        176.99 ms 149.8-152.1 66.7 ms   over 2.28x   was 2.65x
      image-stress      329.75 ms 189.5-189.7 16.7 ms   over 11.4x   was 19.7x

      probe                      before     after     and the control
      image-photo-bicubic      256.4 ms   135.5 ms
      image-photo-bilinear      73.9 ms    48.8 ms
      image-yuv-bilinear       164.7 ms    55.0 ms
      image-widegamut-bilinear  86.4 ms    49.5 ms
      image-photo-mipmapped     74.0 ms    74.0 ms   already decoded, and did not move

  The last row is what says the other four moved for the reason claimed. The three bilinear probes
  now cost within six milliseconds of each other whatever their source encoding is - sRGB, Rec. 2020
  and a three-plane YUV - because after the copy they are the same canonical format.

  SO NEITHER SCENE IS CLOSED, AND WHAT EACH NEEDS IS STILL NAMED. image-stress is now the fetch and
  the arithmetic the probes separated out - 70 ms and 45 of the old bicubic's 254 - plus what a
  sixteen-byte texel costs to walk where a four-byte one used to be, which is the copy's price
  arriving on the other side. UI-effects is where it was: the blur is 49 ms of it and the remaining
  hundred and twenty-eight are spread over forty-five commands, with no dominant term and no probe
  here separating one.

  AND TWO PROBES ALONG THE WAY WERE INVALID AND ARE RECORDED AS SUCH: `Surface::load` and
  `Surface::store` were emptied first and changed nothing, which looked like two clean eliminations.
  `replay` does not call them - it works on a `Tile`, and `target.rs` carries two types with a
  `load`/`store` pair each. An experiment that measures a function nothing calls answers about
  nothing, and it answers confidently.

  SO UI-BASIC'S CEILING IS, ON THIS RENDERER, A BUDGET FOR ONE FULL-FRAME PASS AND 2.15 ms OF
  EVERYTHING ELSE. That reframes what "sits on the line" means and what could move it: the 252
  commands of rectangles, glyphs, images and clips that the scene is NAMED for account for about an
  eighth of its time, so no amount of work on glyph or clip handling reaches the ceiling. What would
  is the per-pixel cost of touching the frame once.
  The other probes bound the rest of the picture: a translucent fullscreen fill is 24.30 ms, a
  hundred small opaque rectangles 19.38 ms, and a single drawn dot in each of eighty tiles 0.08 ms -
  which is the tile-region work from earlier doing exactly what it was for, and is also why a
  compositor updating one damaged corner is not what these four scenes measure.

  ONE HYPOTHESIS ABOUT THOSE 47 NANOSECONDS WAS TESTED AND FAILED, and it is recorded because the
  disproof narrows the search. The transfer encode is the most expensive thing per pixel that the
  store-back does - a square root and an interpolated table lookup per channel - and a solid fill
  hands it the same number for every pixel of a run. A one-entry memo per channel, bit-identical by
  construction, was added and measured: UI-basic 16.89 against 16.85, which is nothing. If the encode
  were the bulk of those 47 nanoseconds, a fullscreen solid fill would have collapsed. It did not, so
  it is not, and the change was reverted rather than kept for the two percent it may have moved on
  the two image-heavy scenes. WHERE THE TIME ACTUALLY GOES IS STILL OPEN, and the next attempt starts
  by knowing it is not there.

  AND THE METHOD CHANGED HALF WAY THROUGH, which is the part worth carrying forward. The first four
  changes were chosen by reasoning about where the time ought to be, and three of them moved almost
  nothing. The benchmark says WHETHER the floor is met and not WHY it is not, so the runner gained a
  probe mode - `SOFT2D_BENCH_PROBE=1` - with scenes small enough to subtract from each other: an
  empty list, one pixel in every tile, one full-screen fill opaque and translucent, a hundred small
  ones, and one full-screen image draw per sampling quality. Everything after that was found by
  asking it.

  WHAT IT FOUND FIRST WAS A SOFTWARE SQUARE ROOT. `sqrt_f32` was four Newton iterations - four
  serially dependent f32 DIVISIONS, a dozen cycles each and impossible to pipeline behind one another
  - and the encode table is indexed by the square root of its input, so it ran three times for EVERY
  PIXEL of every frame. `libm::sqrtf` lowers to the hardware instruction and is correctly rounded
  rather than approximate; it took a full-screen opaque fill from 26.9 ms to 16.2 ms by itself. There
  were TWO copies: `render2d` had a private one whose documentation said "two Newton steps" while its
  loop ran four, on the flattening path. There is one square root in the stack now.

  AND THE SECOND WAS THAT A SAMPLER LOOKED UP ITS ROW PER TEXEL. `read_row` says in as many words
  that "the row lookup is the cost, not the pixel", and the sampler did it four times per pixel for a
  bilinear tap and sixteen for a bicubic one, each time re-deriving the row start from the origin and
  the pitch, asking the storage enum for the minimum row bytes, taking two bounds-checked slices and
  matching the format again. A sampler is built once per image per frame. A full-screen bilinear draw
  went from 97 ms to 82 ms and a bicubic one from 315 ms to 263 ms, and `UI-basic`'s fifty small image
  draws are what took it under its ceiling.

  WHAT THE FOURTEEN WERE, and the first of them is the one this item named and narrowed:
  - A TILE NOTHING READS THE BACKDROP OF IS NOT DECODED. This item said the remaining work was "a
    tile-level path and not a span-level one: a tile that an opaque fill covers entirely should never
    be DECODED into the working space in the first place", and that is done. The conditions are
    narrow deliberately - a solid fully opaque paint at full opacity, `Normal` over or copied, over an
    axis-aligned rectangle, with no clip pushed and no layer open - because each one is a way a pixel
    could otherwise depend on what was under it. Worth 23% of `UI-basic`.
  - A RECTANGULAR CLIP NEEDS NO MASK, and this was not on the list at all. The clip stack has always
    had a rectangle level that costs no storage and no per-pixel multiply - its own module header says
    so - and nothing ever pushed one: every clip rasterised its edges into a full-tile mask, zeroed
    first, in every tile of the frame. A scrolling list of fifty rows did that four thousand times.
    Taken only when the rectangle is PIXEL-ALIGNED, because a rectangle level answers one or zero and
    an edge between two pixel centres has an answer in between.
  - THE WORKING INTERMEDIATE IS FOUR SINGLES AND WAS FOUR HALVES. There is no hardware half
    conversion in reach of a `no_std` build here, so every read and write of a working pixel went
    through a branchy software routine twice per channel. It is MORE accurate rather than less - the
    arithmetic above it was `f32` throughout and the half rounded between every pair of composites -
    and it costs hundreds of kilobytes against a sixty-four megabyte ceiling. Worth 30% of
    `UI-effects`, where a filter graph reads and writes intermediates per node.
  - A ROW IS ONLY AS WIDE AS THE SHAPE REACHED. The area rasteriser zeroed two accumulators across
    the whole width of its bounds, summed them across it, evaluated the winding rule at every column,
    and handed its caller a full-width row to scan for the covered part - while a row of a thin
    stroke crossing a sixty-four-wide tile touches two or three columns. It tracks the columns its
    edges reached, keeps the accumulators clean by zeroing only those, and emits the covered run with
    the index it starts at; the columns outside it have ONE answer each. Worth 8% of `vector-stress`,
    which is the scene made of thin outlines.
  - ONE BOUNDS CHECK PER RUN AND NOT ONE PER CHANNEL. The row converters read each channel with
    `get(index).copied().unwrap_or(0)`, which is four branches and a panic path per pixel that the
    compiler cannot remove, because a chunk whose size is a runtime value could be shorter than index
    three. The two four-byte orders every target here presents are spelt out against
    `chunks_exact(4)`; every other format still goes through the general path. The tile round trip
    fell from 21.0 ms to 17.0 ms.
  - AND ONE SQUARE ROOT FOR THE WHOLE STACK, which is the second copy named above.
  THE CONFORMANCE SUITE IS UNCHANGED BY ALL SEVEN: 112 passed, 0 failed, 0 unsupported, 0 untested,
  on the target. That is the half that had to be true for any of them to be worth having - and the
  square root's replacement is MORE accurate, not less, which is why it could be made at all.
  - A FULLY COVERED OPAQUE RUN IS A COPY. `Cs + Cb * (1 - as)` with `as = 1` is `Cs + Cb * 0`, which
    for any finite backdrop is exactly `Cs`: the backdrop read, the coverage scale and the blend all
    compute a number already in hand. That is the INTERIOR of every filled shape, which is most of a
    drawing's pixels; the edge, where coverage is partial, falls through unchanged.
  - THE DITHER ROW AND THE BLUR'S EDGE, ASKED ONCE INSTEAD OF PER PIXEL - and the blur's second pass
    reads its column as a RUN, where it had walked it with a per-pixel `get` while the first pass had
    used spans for its rows all along.
  - AND A DAMAGE-LIMITED REDRAW NOW COSTS WHAT IT DRAWS, which moves none of these four scenes and is
    the most useful of the eleven. A tile was decoded and re-encoded WHOLE, so a drawing touching
    three pixels of it paid sixty-four rows of conversion; the round trip is now over the union of
    the bounds of the commands binned to that tile, which `prepare` already knows. One pixel in each
    of eighty tiles went from 17.0 ms to 0.081 ms. The four frozen scenes all cover the frame, so
    they see nothing - but a compositor updating one corner is the case the tiling exists for, and it
    was paying the whole frame's conversion to do it. The fixture asserts both halves: the drawing
    arrives whole, and the target outside it is BYTE-IDENTICAL, because a decode and an encode are a
    lossy pair and a pixel that made the trip without needing to may come back different.

  AND WHERE IT STILL IS NOT ENOUGH, NAMED RATHER THAN ESTIMATED, WITH WHAT EACH WOULD TAKE:
  - `UI-basic` is at its ceiling within one percent and `vector-stress` is 14% over. Both are now
    dominated by the TILE ROUND TRIP - a decode and an encode of every pixel touched - against about
    6 ms of actual compositing. That is a per-pixel transfer conversion with a table lookup in it,
    which is the thing that does not vectorise, and closing it means changing what the intermediate
    IS rather than finding another constant factor.
  - `vector-stress` at 1.13x CANNOT REACH ITS CEILING BY SHADER WORK, which the probe settles: the
    same geometry with a free paint is 58.2 ms against a 66.7 ms ceiling, so the linear gradient is
    16 ms of the 75 and removing all of it still leaves only 13% of headroom. What is left is the
    exact-area accumulation over NEAR-HORIZONTAL edges - a stroke of a flat curve has outline edges
    crossing many columns of every row they touch, which is where that rasteriser is most expensive -
    and that is the algorithm rather than its constants. Two divisions per pixel remain in the
    gradient and cannot become multiplications without changing the pixels.
  - `UI-effects` at 3.0x is a DIRECT GAUSSIAN: `2r + 1` taps of a four-channel multiply-add per pixel
    per pass, sixty of them at this scene's sigma. Every constant factor around it has been taken out
    and the arithmetic is what is left; going faster means a box-blur approximation, and the profile
    specifies a Gaussian. That is a change to the profile and not to this backend.
  - `image-stress` at 21x is the fixture question this item already raises and which is the project
    owner's to answer.
  THE ITEM CANNOT BE TICKED ON THREE SCENES, however close two of them are - it asks for all four.

  RE-MEASURED 2026-09-14, AFTER THE EXACT-AREA RASTERISER. That change was made for CORRECTNESS - the
  sampled vertical direction did not meet the frozen coverage threshold, which the item above records
  - and it is also the first of the three things this item named as needed. It is done; the other two
  are not.

  THIS IS A MEASURED GAP AND NOT AN UNKNOWN ONE. `docs/PERF.md` records where the time goes, on the
  same host: an EMPTY list costs 21 ms, which is the tile decode and re-encode of the whole frame and
  nothing else; one full-screen antialiased fill costs 30 ms more, of which 13 ms is the sixteen
  sub-scanlines of coverage and 17 ms is the span composite. Six optimisations already took the four
  scenes between three and six times faster than the first working version, and each was worth making
  on its own terms; what is left is not more of the same.
  WHAT CLOSING IT NEEDS, NAMED RATHER THAN ESTIMATED: an exact-area rasteriser that computes coverage
  in ONE pass instead of sixteen - DONE 2026-09-14, and worth between 1.1x and 2.5x per scene; a span
  composite over more than four scalar lanes; and a specialised path for an opaque solid fill that
  never reads the backdrop. Neither of the remaining two changes WHAT is drawn, and both are ordinary
  work - but they are a body of work with its own risk to the pixel-exact half of the conformance
  contract, and they are not a tail of this item.
  AND THE TILE LOAD AND STORE IS NOW THE LARGEST SINGLE TERM IN `UI-basic`: the decode into the
  working space and the encode back, against a 16.7 ms ceiling. Half of that measurement was a frame
  with NOTHING in it, and that half is gone - a tile no command reaches is no longer replayed at all
  (2026-09-14), which does not move these four scenes because they cover the whole frame and does
  move every damage-limited redraw a compositor performs. What is left is the load and store of the
  tiles a drawing does reach, which is what the opaque-fill path and the tile format question are
  both about.
  AND THE TILE-LEVEL PATH IT NAMED IS NOW DONE - see the 2026-09-15 measurement above.

  ONE OF THE TWO REMAINING OPTIMISATIONS WAS TRIED AND MEASURED AND IS NOT WORTH MAKING (2026-09-14).
  This item named "a span composite over more than four scalar lanes" as one of the two things left.
  Widening `soft2d::span::LANES` from four to eight - the same two operations in the same order, more
  of them at a time, with the bit-for-bit differential test extended to the new tail lengths and
  passing - moved `UI-basic`, `UI-effects` and `image-stress` by less than half a percent and made
  `vector-stress` about three percent SLOWER, on two consecutive runs on the reference host. The
  reason is the measurement already recorded above: the time in these scenes is the tile decode and
  encode, not the span arithmetic, so a wider span loop has nothing to win and pays for the longer
  tail. The constant is back at four with the measurement written beside it, so this is not an idea
  somebody tries again.
  AND THE OPAQUE-SOURCE SHORTCUT IS THE SAME STORY AT THE WRONG LEVEL. A source run whose every pixel
  is fully opaque never reads the backdrop - `Cb * (1 - 1)` is zero for every finite backdrop, so a
  copy is bit-identical to the arithmetic - and skipping the read moved nothing either, for the same
  reason. WHAT THE ITEM MEANT BY "an opaque solid fill that never reads the backdrop" IS THEREFORE A
  TILE-LEVEL PATH AND NOT A SPAN-LEVEL ONE: a tile that an opaque fill covers entirely should never
  be DECODED into the working space in the first place, which is the largest single term in
  `UI-basic` and is where the remaining work is. Narrowed here so the next attempt starts at the
  right level.

  AND ONE OF THE FOUR MAY BE A FIXTURE QUESTION RATHER THAN AN IMPLEMENTATION ONE. `image-stress` has to
  draw, in 16.7 ms at 640x480, eight mipmapped downscales, three upscales at three qualities, thirteen
  wide-gamut conversions and a full-screen `NV12` frame reconstructed, matrixed, transfer-decoded and
  converted from Rec. 2020 - which is about 7.7 million sampled pixels, or under six cycles each on the
  reference host. That is not a number a scalar CPU pipeline reaches, and the scene grew to it when the
  multi-plane model landed (it moved that row from 267 ms to 419 ms). Either the ceiling for a video
  scene is not 60 Hz at this content, or the scene is two scenes; deciding which is a CHANGE TO THIS
  PLAN and its recorded performance contract, which this item says explicitly - so it is a decision for
  the project owner and not something an implementer may quietly adjust.

  **BOTH QUESTIONS WERE ANSWERED (2026-09-21), AND THE ANSWERS CLOSE THE OPTIMISING AND NOT THE BOX.**
  The video scene became TWO scenes on 2026-09-19 - `image-resample` and `image-convert` - and then
  the owner answered the ceiling question for the second of them: TRY to reach it, but NOT at the
  cost of what the backend produces, and if it cannot be reached, LEAVE IT. The four-lane encode was
  asked separately and REFUSED: "it must be universal for every platform", so the three ports stay
  one program with no instruction specific to any of them.

  THE TWO ANSWERS TOGETHER DECIDE IT, because the lever under this item was the output encode and
  there were exactly two ways to pull it: change the pixels, or compute the three channels at once.
  The first is refused by "not at the cost of functionality" and the second by "it must be
  universal". What is left is work that changes no pixel and no instruction set - and the arithmetic
  already under this item says how far that reaches: the per-pixel FLOOR for any covered pixel is
  33 ns, this scene covers the frame TWICE, and `2 x 307,200 x 33 ns` is 20.3 ms against a 16.7 ms
  budget. Sixty frames a second is out of reach at this content with a sampler and a shader that
  cost NOTHING AT ALL, which is an arithmetic verdict rather than a tuning one.

  SO THE OPTIMISING STOPS AND THE BOX STAYS OPEN, and the two are different things. Every remaining
  scene is blocked on something no amount of effort moves:

      scene            over by   what it is blocked on
      UI-basic         met       nothing; it cleared its ceiling
      vector-stress    at line   67.1 to 67.7 against 66.7, and the scene's own run-to-run spread is
                                 wider than anything left to win
      UI-effects       2.28x     a DIRECT GAUSSIAN with every constant factor already out. Going
                                 faster means a box-blur approximation, and the profile SPECIFIES a
                                 Gaussian - so it is a change to `Render2D Core Profile 1`
      image-resample   4.8x      the sampler, which is the scene
      image-convert    7.6x      arithmetic, as above: 20.3 ms of floor against a 16.7 ms ceiling

  THIS ITEM'S OWN RULE IS WHY THE BOX DOES NOT CLOSE: "a first implementation that misses a ceiling
  FAILS `c`, rather than defining a passing baseline", and the runner's budgets stay at the ceilings
  rather than being frozen at what was measured. Closing it means CHANGING A CEILING, which this
  item says is a change to the plan and its recorded performance contract - a decision about what
  this system promises for a video scene, and the one thing "leave it" did not say.

  **RE-MEASURED 2026-09-22, AFTER THE TONE MAP STOPPED RUNNING ON DESTINATIONS THAT CANNOT USE IT.**
  That change is recorded against the HDR item in `f-ext`; what matters here is that it removed a
  per-pixel branch and a luminance dot product from the encode path of every conversion whose
  destination reported no headroom, which is all of them today.

      scene            replay median   ceiling   verdict       (was 2026-09-19)
      UI-basic              11.50 ms   16.7 ms   MET           was 11.81
      vector-stress         67.21 ms   66.7 ms   AT THE LINE   was 66.5-67.0
      UI-effects           147.53 ms   66.7 ms   over 2.21x    was 2.28x
      image-resample        78.06 ms   16.7 ms   over 4.67x
      image-convert        123.96 ms   16.7 ms   over 7.43x

  THE MOVEMENT IS SMALL AND IS REPORTED AS SMALL. UI-effects is about two per cent better and the
  rest is inside the spread these scenes have always had; the measurement was taken to show the
  encode change did not COST anything on the path it sits in, and it did not. `vector-stress` reading
  0.5 ms over its line rather than 0.2 ms under it is the same coin toss this item has already
  described twice, which is why it is still written as AT THE LINE and not as met.

  **AND UI-EFFECTS HAS A DOMINANT TERM AFTER ALL (2026-09-22), WHICH THIS ITEM HAS TWICE SAID IT DOES
  NOT.** That reading - "the blur is 49 ms of it and the remaining hundred and twenty-eight are
  spread over forty-five commands, with no dominant term and no probe here separating one" - was a
  reading of a number nothing had taken apart. Six probes take it apart now, each a piece of the
  scene and nothing else, so a difference between two rows is one term:

      probe                      replay     the difference says
      effects-base                 9.9 ms   the full-screen fill alone
      effects-image               44.3 ms   the 512-square bilinear draw costs 34.4
      effects-layers-plain        15.1 ms   ten layers WITHOUT filters cost 5.2
      effects-layers-blur         51.8 ms   their Gaussian costs 36.7
      effects-layers-filtered     97.3 ms   the OTHER FOUR NODES cost 45.5
      effects-backdrop            29.3 ms   the frosted panel's own blur

  THE FOUR TRIVIAL NODES COST MORE THAN THE TWENTY-FIVE-TAP GAUSSIAN THEY SURROUND. `Offset`,
  `Flood`, `In` and `Composite` are a shifted fetch, a constant fill, a multiply by alpha and a
  source-over, and together they are 45.5 ms against the blur's 36.7 for the same ten layers - 211
  nanoseconds a pixel for work the rest of this backend does in 4.7.

  AND THE OBVIOUS FIX WAS TRIED AND MADE IT WORSE, which is recorded because the disproof is what
  narrows the search. Every one of those nodes walks pixels with `get` and `set` while the blur reads
  and writes RUNS - and the blur's own comment says a per-pixel fetch through a bounds check, a row
  lookup and a half-float decode is most of the cost of the loop around it. Moving all five onto
  spans, with a third run added for the nodes that read two inputs, is bit-identical by construction
  and passes the conformance suite unchanged. It cost 15 ms: the filtered layers went from 97.3 to
  112.8 and the scene from 147.5 to 174.3. A layer here is 124 pixels wide, and three buffered row
  round trips per row of that length are more work than the per-pixel path they replace. Reverted.

  SO WHAT IS EXPENSIVE IN THOSE FOUR NODES IS STILL OPEN, and the next attempt starts by knowing it
  is not the span access - and that the scene cannot reach 66.7 ms even with all four of them free,
  because 36.7 of Gaussian plus 29.3 of backdrop blur is 66 on its own. That is the same wall this
  item already names: the ceiling needs the blur to be cheaper, and a cheaper blur is a box
  approximation, which is a change to `Render2D Core Profile 1` and not an optimisation.

  WHAT THIS ITEM IS WAITING FOR IS UNCHANGED and it is not an optimisation. `image-convert` has an
  arithmetic proof above that it cannot reach 16.7 ms without changing a pixel or an instruction set,
  which the owner's own answer forbids; what is open there is the CEILING, which is a statement about
  what this system promises at that content and which this item refuses to let an implementer pick.

## P02M0103d - 2D conformance and 2D Test SW

The gate that says Profile 1 is real. Nothing in the 3D track needs it. In-tree headless consumers
may exercise the `b`/`c` foundation; a complete presenting 2D application additionally passes this
part, which depends on `c` and `a-wsi`.

- [x] **`soft2d` DID NOT MEET THE COVERAGE THRESHOLD THIS MILESTONE FROZE, and now does** (found
  2026-09-13 by the text guest gate, which compares a drawn frame against an ANALYTIC oracle rather
  than against a captured baseline; fixed 2026-09-14).
  THE NUMBERS. `graphics-profile::thresholds` freezes render2d antialiasing coverage at `<= 2/255`
  absolute per pixel against the analytic area, with `<= 1/255` mean over the covered region.
  `soft2d`'s rasteriser is EXACT IN X AND SAMPLED IN Y: each pixel row is cut into sixteen
  sub-scanlines and the crossings on each are computed exactly, so a vertical edge is analytic and a
  near-horizontal one is quantised to a sixteenth of a level. Half a step is a thirty-second, which
  is `8/255` - four times the frozen bound. The measurement: an axis-aligned rectangle whose top edge
  falls at `y = 26.4` is drawn with coverage `10/16 = 0.625` where the area is `0.6`, a difference of
  `6/255`, and the pixels a VERTICAL edge cuts agree to the last bit.
  THE RASTERISER'S OWN COMMENT SAYS THE SAMPLING "buys accuracy the tolerance does not ask for",
  which is the claim this measurement contradicts. One of the two is wrong and the specification is
  the one that was frozen, so the implementation is what moves.
  THE FIX IS NOT MORE SAMPLES. Reaching `2/255` by sampling needs 128 sub-scanlines, which is eight
  times the work on a path that already has a performance floor it does not meet. The answer is an
  ANALYTIC AREA rasteriser - the signed-area cell accumulation every serious 2D rasteriser uses -
  which is exact in both directions AND cheaper than sixteen sweeps, so this item and the performance
  floor above it are the same change rather than competing ones.
  DONE 2026-09-14. THE RASTERISER ACCUMULATES AREA. Each edge is clipped to the pixel row it crosses
  and then to each pixel column it passes through, and two numbers are accumulated per pixel: the
  signed vertical extent the edge spans there, and the area of that pixel lying to the RIGHT of it. A
  left-to-right sweep of the row turns the pair into the winding-weighted coverage of every pixel,
  exactly, at any sub-pixel position, in both directions. The fill rule is applied to that accumulated
  value - saturated for non-zero, folded for even-odd - rather than to a per-sub-scanline winding.
  THE ALIASED PATH IS STILL A SAMPLE AT THE PIXEL'S CENTRE, and has to be: "a pixel is in or it is
  out" is a different question from "how much of it is covered", and thresholding an area gives a
  different answer for a sliver narrower than half a pixel.
  WHAT IT IS HELD TO, in `soft2d`'s own fixtures rather than only in the guest run: an axis-aligned
  rectangle of fractional size is drawn at nine offsets walking a whole pixel in both axes, and every
  pixel is compared against the product of its two one-dimensional overlaps - exactly where the
  analytic coverage is 0 or 1, and within the frozen 2/255 and 1/255 elsewhere. A second fixture
  draws a long shallow triangle, whose upper edge crosses one pixel row every six columns, and holds
  the drawing's total ink to its own area - which is where a quantised near-horizontal edge loses
  systematically rather than randomly. Quantising the accumulated value to sixteenths makes the first
  fixture fail at 4.2/255, so it is a live comparison rather than a passing one.
  AND THE MEASUREMENT THAT FOUND IT NOW READS 0.49/255 where it read 6/255: the text guest gate
  applies the frozen bound in both directions and reports which one a regression is in.
  IT ALSO MADE THE RENDERER FASTER, which the performance item below predicted: sixteen sweeps each
  with a sort of its crossings became one pass. `vector-stress` 245.8 -> 97.0 ms, `UI-basic`
  78.8 -> 46.4 ms, `UI-effects` 385.1 -> 332.0 ms, `image-stress` 419.0 -> 377.7 ms, on the same host
  with the same frozen fixtures.

- [x] Build a 2D CONFORMANCE suite as a governed PIE named **2D Conformance SW**, canonical artifact
  `test2d-conformance-sw.lsexe`, so it is named and auditable on the same terms as the 3D one rather
  than being "a suite" with no artifact. It states its grants, its deterministic control protocol,
  its result format, its timeout, how a failing feature is identified by name, and its architecture
  matrix. It walks the profile entry by entry - driven by the machine-readable registry, so a feature
  added to the profile without a test here is a gate failure - with a scene per feature and a stated
  pass condition per scene rather than one screenshot. It runs on all three
  architectures, tolerance-based where floating point demands it and exact where the bit-exact half
  applies, and it FAILS if any profile entry reports `Unsupported`. Coverage: both fill rules,
  curves, every cap/join/dash, projective transforms, all four paint kinds with every spread mode,
  rect/rounded/path/nested/inverse clipping, layer nesting and group opacity, every Porter-Duff
  operator, every separable and non-separable blend mode, nearest/bilinear/bicubic and minification,
  every filter node and a composed graph, backdrop filtering, wide-gamut and linear composition,
  HDR-to-SDR output with dithering, path boolean operations and hit testing, and every glyph kind
  including a colour emoji.

  DONE 2026-09-14. `user/libs/graphics/conformance2d` is the suite and `bin/test2d-conformance-sw.lsexe`
  is the program around it - one hundred and ten scenes, one per profile entry, each with its own
  stated pass condition.

  IT IS A LIBRARY WITH A PROGRAM AROUND IT, which is what lets the SAME scenes run as host tests on
  the machine that builds the tree and inside a booted guest on each architecture. A suite that
  existed only as a program would be checked once a boot; one that existed only as a host test would
  never make the three-architecture claim at all.

  DRIVEN BY THE REGISTRY AND NOT BY A LIST OF SCENES SOMEBODY WROTE. Each scene carries a
  `@covers:` marker, the generator walks the profile and refuses a name the profile does not have,
  and the run itself reports the entries with no scene by name - so a feature added to the profile
  with no test is a failure in two places rather than a feature nobody notices. The gate went from
  "conformance coverage NOT PERFORMED, 110 features await a test" to "every one of the 110 features
  has at least one conformance test" and cannot go back quietly.

  THE PASS CONDITIONS ARE ARITHMETIC AND NOT SCREENSHOTS. The target is LINEAR, so a stored byte is
  the value times 255 and a scene states "half multiplied by four fifths is two fifths" rather than
  comparing against a recorded image - which would agree with this backend by construction. One
  drawing tells all thirteen operators apart: an opaque backdrop, a six-tenths source and three
  probes. SIX TENTHS AND NOT A HALF, because at a half `as` and `1 - as` are the same number and four
  pairs of operators become indistinguishable - which is how a suite passes a backend that has `Xor`
  and `DestinationAtop` the wrong way round. Every blend mode is its equation at one point with a
  different value in each channel; the four non-separable ones are checked as their DEFINITION - the
