  `render3d` does - a scene is built from application data, and unbounded application data reaching a
  renderer is the shape every other part of this tree bounds: maximum nodes, hierarchy depth, mesh
  instances, lights per type, visible draws and passes, each with a stated refusal on overflow. With
  the structural checks beside them: CYCLE DETECTION in the hierarchy and in the pass graph, and
  dirty-flag propagation and update ordering. `f-ext` extends the same limit structure with its own
  fields rather than introducing a second one.

- [x] Transparency is the library's job, not the caller's. An opaque queue and an alpha-mask queue,
  with per-draw blend state and colour write masks. A sorted transparent queue is `f-ext`; what is
  not acceptable is telling the application to sort its own geometry and calling that a design
  decision, which is why `f-ext` owns it rather than nobody.
  DONE 2026-09-14, AND THE SORTED TRANSPARENT QUEUE IS HERE RATHER THAN IN `f-ext`: the frozen core
  profile puts all three queues in the core with their ordering rules, so deferring the third would
  be claiming conformance to a document this layer does not implement. Opaque and AlphaMask are
  front to back and write depth; Transparent is back to front and does not, because writing it would
  make a transparent surface hide the one behind it. The sort key is the distance from the camera
  POSITION to the world bounding sphere's CENTRE - not its nearest point, which makes a large object
  sort ahead of a small one it contains - and the tiebreak is submission order, running the SAME way
  in the reversed queue so two objects at one distance cannot swap between queues.
  Per-draw blend state and colour write masks are the material's, and are checked against its
  blending.

- [x] Picking and readback, because a desktop 3D application, an editor and a CAD viewport all need
  them: an object-ID attachment, a selection pass, and depth and pixel readback. The software backend
  may return an already-completed ticket; a future GPU backend returns a pending ticket through
  the submission/readback contract `e` defines, without changing the API shape.
  DONE 2026-09-14. A drawable carries a 32-bit identity the scene assigns and the application may
  set, with ZERO RESERVED FOR NOTHING so a pick on the background is unambiguous, and two drawables
  claiming one identity are refused. A transparent drawable writes no identity, so a pick through
  glass answers what is behind it. The selection pass writes the identity BESIDE the colour and the
  depth in ONE pass: a separate pass would depth-test against its own buffer and could answer
  something the person cannot see.
  `Readback` is `Identity`, `Depth` or `Colour` - an editor wants the first, a CAD viewport the
  second to place a cursor in the world, a colour picker the third - and all three read the same
  pass through one completion contract. `Pending` carries the submission serial rather than a value,
  and `is_answered_by` is what a software backend satisfies immediately and a GPU backend later,
  with nothing about the shape changing. A depth readback asked to name a drawable is REFUSED, not
  reinterpreted: a depth value read as an identity names whichever drawable holds that number, which
  is wrong and looks right. A pick outside the attachment is refused rather than clamped, because a
  clamped pick answers about a pixel the caller did not ask about.
  AND THE SAME ANSWER IS AVAILABLE WITHOUT A FRAME: `pick::resolve` casts a ray against the
  drawables that write an identity and finds the nearest, which is what the attachment would have
  held. The ray is built from TWO unprojected points rather than an eye and a direction, because
  under an orthographic projection every ray is parallel and the camera position is on none of them.

- [x] Host tests: hierarchy transform composition, culling against known frusta, queue ordering,
  instancing, picking, limit refusals, hierarchy and pass-graph cycle detection, and that every scene
  feature emits only `render3d` commands.
  DONE 2026-09-14. 52 fixtures, each holding a rule against a value worked out by hand from the
  definition rather than from what the code produces. The frustum is a 90-degree square one whose
  half-width equals its depth, so every culling case is arithmetic a reader can check; one fixture
  asserts the PLANE ORDER is observable by putting a sphere outside two planes at once and requiring
  the first in the fixed order to be the one named. The emission fixture builds a scene that uses the
  hierarchy, a camera on a node, a light, masks, a disabled subtree, culling, all three queues, a
  64-instance draw, an offscreen target and picking identities, and then requires exactly one draw
  per surviving drawable, one pass, and an identity bound for the two opaque queues and not the
  transparent one.

## P02M0103f-ext - Scene3D Extended: materials, lighting, animation and postprocessing

**ACTIVATED (2026-09-21), BY THE EXPLICIT APPROVAL THIS PART REQUIRED.** The owner was asked
whether `f-ext` is activated and `e` approved, and answered: do them. That is the approval this
header names and nothing else was standing between this part and an implementer.

AND THE PREREQUISITES WERE CHECKED RATHER THAN ASSUMED, WHICH IS THE POINT OF HAVING THEM.
Activation says the part may be built; it does not waive the ordering rules. `P02M0103f` and
`P02M0103g` carry no open item, and `s-3d-ext` is done - `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_1.md` exists, is
GENERATED from the `scene3d_extended` registry rather than written beside it, and carries its own
specification hash, which is what the freeze means here. The freeze came first by several weeks, so
there is nothing for the ordering rule to hold back and the nine items below are ordinary open work.

AND "A SHAPE WITH NO REAL CONSUMER IS A GUESS" STILL APPLIES INSIDE THE PART. The rule was this
part's activation gate and it is also a design rule: each feature below is built against something
that asks for it - a demo, a conformance case, a real scene - rather than against the list. The
approval answers "may this part exist", which was the question; it does not answer "what shape
should each feature have", which the consumers do.

SEPARATELY ACTIVATED, like every other optional part in this file: it is designed and may be
implemented, and it is in no core Done gate.

It carries its own closed `Scene3D Extended Profile 1` in the machine-readable registry, on the same
terms as every other profile here: no `Unsupported` inside it, a test per feature, and stated minima.
A build claims the Extended profile entirely or not at all.

**THAT CLOSED LIST NOW EXISTS (written 2026-09-21), AND WRITING IT WAS THIS PART'S FIRST ACT.** It
did not exist when the part was activated earlier the same day: the registry carried the EQUATIONS -
`PBR_TERMS`, `PBR_CONSTANTS`, `ENVIRONMENT_RULES`, `SHADOW_RULES`, `POSTPROCESS_RULES`,
`ANIMATION_RULES` and `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_1_MIN_LIMITS` - while the CORE carried a `Scene3DFeature`
enum and `SCENE3D_CORE_PROFILE_1` beside them, and `scene3d_extended` had no enum and no list.

WHAT WAS WRITTEN: `ExtendedFeature`, 52 variants in seven groups - material 11, environment 6,
shadows 10, postprocess 6, animation 11, detail 6, limits 2 - and `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_PROFILE_1` over
them, through the same `profile!` macro the other three lists use, with `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_GROUPS`,
`in_extended_profile_1` and `entry_by_name` to match. The specification hash moved from `198e9d18`
to `ef2117e5`, which is the amendment being made LOUDLY rather than quietly; the list is hashed
together with the equations, because a list that could change without moving the hash would be a
second statement of the profile that nothing holds to the first.

EVERY VARIANT COMES FROM A RULE THAT WAS ALREADY FROZEN, EXCEPT LEVEL OF DETAIL - see the paragraph
below, which is the defect this part was flagged for and which the same amendment closed.

WHAT IT DOES NOT DO is implement any of the 52. It makes them NAMEABLE, which is what every item
below needs before it can claim to cover one.

WHY THAT IS A DECISION AND NOT AN AFTERNOON. `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_1.md` is GENERATED from those
registries and carries a specification hash, and `s-3d-ext` froze it. Adding a closed feature list
means adding it to the registry and emitting it into the document, which moves the hash - so the
first item of this part is an AMENDMENT to a frozen normative specification. The ordering rules this
file states everywhere exist to stop an implementer doing that quietly, and every other item's "a
test per feature" refers to the list that amendment would create.

WHAT `s-3d-ext` ACTUALLY FROZE, read back rather than assumed: its own words are "the Extended
feature EQUATIONS, LIMITS, TOLERANCES and LIFECYCLE CHOICES", and all four are in the registry and
in the document. So the freeze is satisfied as written and the closed list is this part's own
deliverable - which is exactly why it lands on the frozen document rather than before it.

AND ONE ITEM BELOW ASKED FOR SOMETHING THE FROZEN DOCUMENT DID NOT STATE AT ALL: "LOD selection with
STATED THRESHOLDS". There were no LOD thresholds anywhere in `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_1.md` - no screen
coverage, no distance, no hysteresis - so that half of its item could not be implemented against a
stated rule, only against an invented one. It was the same shape as `max_transparent_items`: a line
written before the split and not carried into the freeze.

CLOSED BY THE SAME AMENDMENT (2026-09-21), because a feature with no stated rule cannot go in a
closed list and the list was this part's first deliverable. `LEVEL_OF_DETAIL_RULES` now answers nine
questions: the selection metric is SCREEN COVERAGE - the world bounding sphere's projected radius as
a fraction of half the viewport height - rather than raw distance, which picks a different level at
the same apparent size when the field of view or the viewport changes; the ladder is declared most
detailed first and REFUSED at load if it is not strictly descending; the default ladder is 0.5,
0.25, 0.125, 0.0625; hysteresis is a tenth of the threshold applied to the level held last frame,
which is what stops a drawable sitting on a boundary swapping mesh every frame; the first frame has
no previous level and reads the ladder directly; below the last threshold the last level keeps being
drawn unless the mesh declares a coverage at which it vanishes; a mesh with one level never consults
a threshold; the coverage is taken from the CURRENT bounds, after morphing and skinning, because a
rest-pose coverage steps a raised arm down a level while it is still on screen; and the chosen level
is READABLE, which is the difference from culling - culling must not change the picture and a level
of detail is chosen to.

They are CHOSEN RATHER THAN REQUIRED, on the same terms as the root-motion rule, which is what
`s-3d-ext`'s own criterion asks for: "the choices its own rows leave open have one answer each". A
seventh minimum limit, `max_lod_levels` at 4, came with them and is enforced in `scene3d::Limits`
beside the six. `max_transparent_items` is still left out, for the reason already given: a
transparent item is a drawable and `max_drawables` bounds it, and moving a frozen hash is for a rule
the profile is missing rather than for a second name over a bound that exists.

AND A NORMATIVE PROFILE OF ITS OWN NEEDS A FREEZE OF ITS OWN, WHICH THIS PART DID NOT HAVE (added
2026-09-04). This file's governing rule is that a normative specification is frozen before the part
that implements it, and `s` previously defined only the four core freeze points - `s-common`, `s-2d`, `s-3d` and `s-wsi` -
none of which covers `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_1.md`; the `s-3d` Done clause names the CORE
`SCENE3D_PROFILE_1.md` and says the Extended document is outside it. So this part could be approved
and started with its own normative profile still mutable, which is the exact ordering defect those
gates exist to prevent, and it is the same omission `s-wsi` was added for on 2026-09-01.

`s-3d-ext` is the fifth freeze point: it is done when `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_1.md` exists with its registry
entry on the same terms as every other profile here, the five requirements the `s` section states hold
for it, and the choices its own rows leave open have one answer each. It is a prerequisite of `f-ext`
and of nothing else - no core Done clause waits for it, which is what keeps an optional part optional.

- [x] LOD selection with stated thresholds, skinning, morph targets, and dynamic bounds recomputed
  for skinned and morphed geometry rather than trusting the rest pose.

  DONE 2026-09-21: the arithmetic, the profile's thresholds, the scene-layer wiring, the conformance
  scenes and - last and only after a fixture went looking for it - the level reaching a draw.

  THE ARITHMETIC IS IMPLEMENTED (2026-09-21) IN THREE MODULES OF `scene3d`, WITH A FIXTURE PER RULE.
  `detail` carries the ladder, the coverage metric and the hysteresis; `deform` carries linear blend
  skinning, morph targets, the composed joint matrices and `deformed_bounds`, which is where MORPH
  THEN SKIN lives so the order is not open-coded wherever bounds are wanted; `animate` carries clips,
  interpolation, root motion and the loop-seam refusal. 24 fixtures, 57 to 81 in the crate.

  THE STATED THRESHOLDS EXIST NOW BECAUSE THIS PART WROTE THEM. They did not exist when the item was
  written, which is what the paragraph above the list records; the closed feature list could not
  name a feature with no stated rule, so the rules and the list went in together and the hash moved.

  AND THE SCENE LAYER USES IT NOW (2026-09-21). `Drawable` carries the LADDER, because a ladder
  belongs to the mesh and is the same in every view; `detail::ViewDetail` carries the LEVEL HELD
  LAST FRAME, one per view, because coverage is a function of where the camera is. That split is
  the answer to the question this paragraph used to record as open: two cameras looking at one scene
  pick different levels for one drawable, and a single cached level would make each view's
  hysteresis overwrite the other's - a flicker that appears only once a second view exists. The type
  makes it impossible rather than documenting it.

  `detail::select` mirrors `queue::build` and reads the camera's shape OUT OF ITS PROJECTION MATRIX
  rather than out of the fields it was built from, for the same reason `cull` extracts its planes
  that way. `animate::apply` drives nodes through a `Skeleton`, and a channel the clip does not
  drive is left alone - writing an identity into it would make a clip that only rotates a wrist also
  move it to the origin.

  AND THE CONFORMANCE SUITE WALKS IT NOW (2026-09-21). `conformance3d` carries `EXTENDED_CASES`, 60
  scenes for 60 entries, walked by the same `run` the two core halves use and behind the same
  untested-feature check: a feature added to the profile with no scene is a failure of the suite.

  **AND THE LEVEL REACHES A DRAW NOW (2026-09-21), WHICH IT DID NOT WHEN THE PARAGRAPH ABOVE WAS
  WRITTEN.** `detail::select` filled a `ViewDetail` and nothing read it: `queue::build` sorted by
  material and `emit::record` drew `drawable.mesh`, so a scene could choose a coarse level every
  frame and still draw the finest mesh for ever. A feature whose output nothing consumes is not
  implemented, and the fixture that would have said so - the one ranging over every Extended feature
  - is the sixth clause of the host-test item below, which had been ticked without being written.

  THE LEVEL IS RESOLVED INTO THE QUEUE AND NOT AT THE DRAW, because the queue is already the
  per-view list and the level is already a per-view answer: `Queued` carries the MESH THIS ENTRY
  DRAWS, resolved once where the entry is made, and the recorder reads it. Resolving it in the
  recorder would mean a second per-view structure threaded through a function whose whole input is
  otherwise the queue - and two per-view answers is how a view comes to sort one mesh and draw
  another.

  AND A VANISHED DRAWABLE NEVER REACHES A QUEUE. `Detail::Vanished` is the ladder saying the object
  is too small to be worth a draw call, so it is dropped where a culled one is and counted in its
  own tally beside `culled` - a vanished drawable counted as culled would make the two numbers one,
  and they answer different questions: how much geometry the frustum rejected, and how much the
  ladder did.

  A THIRD TALLY AND NOT A THIRD SET OF NUMBERS IN THE FIRST TWO. `Summary::complete()` deliberately
  leaves Extended out, because the profile is OPTIONAL AS A WHOLE and folding it in would make
  "conforms" mean something the profile does not say; `complete_with_extended()` is the separate
  claim, and the guest program prints the two on separate lines so a reader can tell a
  core-conforming implementation WITHOUT the part from one that claims it and fails it.

- [x] `PbrMetallicRoughness` over the programmable pipeline. The PBR material carries base-colour,
  metallic-roughness, normal, occlusion and emissive maps, `Opaque`/`Mask`/`Blend` alpha modes and a
  double-sided flag - which is the glTF data model, chosen deliberately as the reality check, because
  a material model that cannot consume the assets the world already has is a material model for one
  demo.

  THE DIRECT TERM IS IMPLEMENTED (2026-09-21) in `scene3d`'s `pbr` module, with ten fixtures whose
  expected values are computed by hand from the equations - and the three terms asserted separately
  beside the sum, so a failure says WHICH of the four moved. `PbrMaterial` is a SEPARATE TYPE and
  not a fifth `MaterialKind`: the core list is four entries and adding to that enum would change
  what `Scene3D Core Profile 1` means.

  AND THE THINGS THIS PARAGRAPH SAYS THE PROFILE MUST PICK, IT NOW PICKS. The frozen registry had
  the equations and none of the inputs - no occlusion rule, no emissive units, no normal-map
  convention, no per-map colour space - which is the state this item calls "the material is only a
  name". `PBR_MATERIAL_RULES` answers nine of them and the hash moved with it.

  WHAT IS LEFT: the environment half and the emit path. The item names "the environment prefiltering
  and BRDF LUT", and those belong to the item below rather than to this one; the direct term is
  complete without them and an environment lookup is not. Nothing in `Scene`'s own emit path
  constructs a PBR material yet - the level of detail and the animation are wired and the material
  is not, because a material reaches the backend as a PIPELINE and that is `render3d` work rather
  than a scene-layer join.

  AND THE CONFORMANCE HALF IS NO LONGER OUTSTANDING (2026-09-21): `conformance3d::extended` carries
  a case per Extended feature - detail, animation, material, environment, shadows, postprocess and
  the limits - and `complete_with_extended` is the claim a layer makes when every one of them passes.

  WHAT IS LEFT OF THIS ITEM IS THE MATERIAL'S OWN JOIN, AND IT IS A DECISION RATHER THAN AN
  AFTERNOON. `Material` carries a `MaterialKind`, and the core profile's list is exactly four -
  `Unlit`, `VertexColor`, `Lambert`, `BlinnPhong`. `PbrMaterial` is deliberately a SEPARATE type so
  that list stays four, which means a scene cannot hold one in the table the emit path reads: giving
  it one of the four kinds would put a lie in the inventory, and adding a fifth would change what
  `Scene3D Core Profile 1` means. What a scene needs is a SECOND material table that only a layer
  claiming Extended has, and `material_of` resolving a drawable into either - which is a scene-layer
  shape this part has not stated and which this file's own rule says is written before it is built.
  The arithmetic and every parameter it needs are done and fixtured; what is missing is where the
  material LIVES.

  **THE DECISION, TAKEN 2026-09-21, AND THE SHAPE IT PICKS.** A drawable names ONE material in ONE of
  TWO TABLES, and which table is part of the name rather than a flag beside it: `Drawable.material`
  is a `MaterialRef`, either `Core(index)` into the four-kind table or `Extended(index)` into the PBR
  one. A drawable cannot carry both and cannot carry neither, which is what a pair of an index and an
  optional second index would have allowed - and the ambiguous case in that shape is a drawable whose
  core index is read by one pass and whose PBR index is read by another, drawn as two different
  surfaces in one frame.

  `Drawable::new` KEEPS ITS SIGNATURE AND MEANS CORE, and `Drawable::extended` is the other
  constructor. Sixty-four core call sites in the conformance suite and the fixtures say
  `Drawable::new(node, mesh, material)` today and say the same thing afterwards; more than that, a
  program that implements only the core profile never spells `MaterialRef` at all, which is what "the
  core stays unaware of the part" means in code rather than in a sentence.

  THE SECOND TABLE IS `ExtendedMaterial`, WHICH IS `PbrMaterial` PLUS THE BACKEND BINDING - a
  pipeline, a uniform block and a blend state - because that is exactly what `Material` is for the
  core four, and a table the emit path can read has to answer the same five questions the recorder
  asks: which pipeline, which uniforms, which queue, does it write depth, does it write an identity.
  `pbr::PbrMaterial` stays the pure factors with its ten hand-computed fixtures; nothing about the
  equations moves.

  AND THE QUEUE RULE IS MOVED RATHER THAN COPIED. `queue()`, `writes_depth()` and `writes_id()` are
  functions of `Blending` and of nothing else, so they become methods on `Blending` and BOTH material
  types delegate to them. Writing them a second time on the Extended type is how the two families
  come to disagree about what a transparent surface does, which is a bug that appears as a PBR
  surface hiding what is behind it while the core one does not.

  `material_of` RESOLVES INTO EITHER AND RETURNS A `Shading<'_>`, which answers those five questions
  for both. The three callers - `emit::record_one`, `queue::build` and `pick::at` - ask the enum and
  not the table, so neither of them grows a branch per material family.

  THE CEILING IS THE CORE `max_materials` AND NO SEVENTH LIMIT IS ADDED, for the reason
  `max_transparent_items` was refused above: a PBR material IS a material, the core profile already
  bounds how many a scene may hold, and a second bound on one thing is two numbers that can disagree.
  The two tables are counted TOGETHER against it.

  AND A SCENE THAT DOES NOT CLAIM EXTENDED HAS NO SECOND TABLE. `add_extended_material` refuses with
  `Error::NotExtended` when `limits.claims_extended()` is false, which is the whole-or-nothing rule
  the six limits already state, applied at the one entry point that would otherwise let a layer
  acquire half the part by adding a material to it.

  **AND IT IS BUILT (2026-09-21), AND THE FIVE MAPS ARE NAMED WITH IT.** `ExtendedMaterial` is
  `PbrMaterial` plus a pipeline, a uniform block, a blend state and `PbrMaps` - the caller's own
  identifiers for base colour, metallic-roughness, normal, occlusion and emissive, because a
  material a backend can draw has to say WHICH texture each of the five is and `PbrSurface` carries
  only what was read out of them. `None` is a map the material does not have, and its absence is the
  identity: a material with no maps is the same path as one with five.

  AND THE COLOUR SPACE OF EACH MAP IS A FUNCTION RATHER THAN A SENTENCE. `PbrMap::semantics` answers
  `Color` for base colour and emissive and `Data` for the other three, which is the frozen rule made
  executable - the one rule that costs nothing to get wrong and changes every pixel, since decoding
  a normal map bends every normal toward the surface and decoding a roughness map makes a whole
  material glossier, and neither reports itself.

  SEVEN FIXTURES HOLD THE JOIN: the two tables and their separate indices, the refusal on a scene
  that does not claim Extended, the one ceiling over both, the per-table "no such material", the
  shared queue rule across both families with the pick passing through a blended PBR surface, the
  factors that are not mixes, and the five maps with their colour spaces. 138 fixtures in the crate.

  WHAT IS STILL NOT HERE, STATED SO THE TICK IS NOT READ AS MORE THAN IT IS: no BACKEND draws this
  material. The scene layer names a pipeline and a uniform block and records the binds; building a
  GGX pipeline and sampling five maps is `render3d` and `soft3d` work, which this item's own text
  puts outside it ("a material reaches the backend as a PIPELINE").

  "PBR metallic-roughness" is a family of implementations that disagree, so this profile picks: GGX /
  Trowbridge-Reitz normal distribution, Smith visibility, Schlick Fresnel, the roughness remapping
  and its minimum, the metallic workflow's base-colour interpretation, how occlusion is applied,
  the units of emissive, tangent-space handedness and the normal-map convention, how a double-sided
  surface flips its normal, the alpha-mask threshold, the environment prefiltering and BRDF LUT, and
  the colour space of EVERY map - base colour and emissive are `Color`, metallic-roughness, normal
  and occlusion are `Data` and are never transfer-decoded. Without those, a conformance scene has no
  single expected answer and the material is only a name.

- [x] Environment maps, image-based lighting, and shadow maps: directional, spot, and cube shadows
  for point lights. With the parameters that give a conformance scene one expected answer: the
  shadow-map format, the constant and slope-scaled bias, the PCF kernel, the cube-face orientation
  for point lights, and whether directional cascades are inside this profile or outside it. Plus a
  shadow-resource budget in `Scene3DLimits`.

  THE ARITHMETIC IS IMPLEMENTED (2026-09-21) in `scene3d`'s `environment` and `shadow` modules, and
  every parameter this item asks to be named was ALREADY named by `s-3d-ext` - so unlike the level
  of detail and the material's inputs, this one needed no amendment to the frozen profile.

  BOTH HALVES OF THE SPLIT SUM ARE GENERATED FROM THE SAME `pbr` TERMS THE DIRECT LIGHTING USES,
  which is what the profile's "generated by the same GGX and Smith terms above" means literally:
  `brdf_integration` calls `pbr::visibility_smith` rather than the second Smith with its own `k`
  that the usual image-based-lighting derivation substitutes. Two Smith terms in one renderer is one
  material for its lights and another for its sky.

  THE TWO INVARIANTS THE FIXTURES REST ON: a sky of uniform radiance 1 delivers an irradiance of
  exactly `pi` to a surface of any orientation (measured 3.1416056), and prefiltering a uniform
  environment returns it unchanged at every roughness. Each catches a whole class of error that no
  single rendered image reveals.

  **AND THE RESOURCES AND THE CASTER PASS ARE IN (2026-09-21).** `shadow::cascade_map_desc` and
  `cube_map_desc` describe what a light's map IS - a `Depth32F` array of one layer per cascade, or six
  cube faces - and `environment::cube_desc` and `hdr_target_desc` describe the other two.
  `emit::record_casters` renders into them.

  NO MIPS ON A SHADOW MAP, which is the half worth writing down: a mip is an AVERAGE of depths, and
  the average of a near depth and a far one is a depth nothing in the scene is at - so a filtered
  shadow map compares against a surface that does not exist. An ARRAY for cascades and a CUBE for a
  point light, because the lighting pass picks one layer per fragment by its own view depth and one
  face per fragment by a direction, and both are selections the sampler can take.

  THE CASTER PASS DOES NOT DRAW THE TRANSPARENT QUEUE, and that is the profile's shape rather than a
  simplification: a surface that lets light through does not stop it, and a map with one depth per
  texel has no way to say "some of the light got past" - so a transparent caster written into it
  casts a SOLID shadow. The masked queue gets a pipeline of ITS OWN or is left out entirely, because
  a leaf drawn as a solid quad is worse than no leaf shadow. And the depth write is FORCED, read out
  of `emit::caster_state` rather than off the recorded list - `Command::BindPipeline` carries the
  pipeline and not the state it was validated against, so the rule is only testable where it is
  decided.

  `max_shadow_maps` and `max_shadow_cascades` were already in `Scene3DLimits` and already refuse; the
  descriptions ask them, so a cascade count above the ceiling is refused where the resource is
  described rather than where it is used.

  DONE 2026-09-21. Every parameter this item asks to be named is named by the frozen profile, the
  arithmetic computes them, the resources describe themselves, the caster pass records them, and the
  demo phase draws one - a directional light's map, rendered and sampled, in a program a person can
  run.

  WHAT THE TICK DOES NOT CLAIM, STATED SO IT IS NOT READ AS MORE: no BACKEND samples a shadow map on
  its own. `Render3D Core Profile 1` has no depth-texture binding and no comparison sampler, which
  the demo phase discovered by needing one - it writes light-space depth into a colour attachment and
  reads it back between passes. A comparison sampler is a profile question and belongs to whoever
  opens the render profile next, not to a part that is optional by construction.

- [x] Animation belongs to Extended alongside skinning and morph targets; core does not require a
  skeletal pose input or an animation player. The clip data model and playback here are: `AnimationClip` with channels, translation/rotation/scale
  tracks and morph-weight tracks; step, linear and cubic interpolation; loop, clamp and ping-pong;
  cross-fade and blending between several clips; skeleton pose evaluation; and a stated root-motion
  policy. Asset importers stay deferred - reading a glTF file is not this milestone - but the runtime
  that plays a clip is what makes the scene layer usable without one.

  PART OF IT IS IMPLEMENTED (2026-09-21) in `scene3d`'s `animate` and `deform` modules: `Clip` with
  translation, rotation and scale tracks, LINEAR interpolation for translation and scale, SPHERICAL
  LINEAR along the shorter arc for rotation, LOOP and CLAMP, the root-motion policy, the loop-seam
  refusal at load, and skeleton pose evaluation with linear blend skinning and morph targets.

  AND THE REST OF THE ITEM'S LIST IS IMPLEMENTED TOO, after a fourth amendment of the same shape as
  the three before it. `ANIMATION_RULES` fixed the interpolation as linear and spherical-linear and
  the ends as looping or not, and said nothing about STEP or CUBIC interpolation, PING-PONG,
  CROSS-FADE, or MORPH-WEIGHT tracks - all of which this item names. `ANIMATION_PLAYBACK_RULES` now
  answers eleven questions and nine features joined the closed list with them:

    - the mode is PER TRACK, because one mode for a whole clip is what makes an author fake the
      others with extra keys.
    - the cubic is HERMITE with AUTHORED tangents, not Catmull-Rom: a derived tangent changes when a
      neighbouring key moves, so editing one key would alter a curve three keys away.
    - a cubic ROTATION is component-wise and then normalised, NOT slerped - and the rule says why
      that is not a contradiction of the linear rule, which refuses exactly that. A cubic through
      four control points has no spherical form at all.
    - PING-PONG's turning frames are visited ONCE per period. Holding the last frame across two
      frames is a stutter at both ends, once a cycle, for the life of the clip.
    - a target only one clip drives is taken from it UNCHANGED. Scaling it by its clip's weight
      would pull the joint toward the origin as the blend moves away, which is a limb collapsing.
    - root motion under a blend is the same weighted sum: a cross-fade from a walk to a run gives a
      speed BETWEEN them, where adding the deltas gives a character outrunning both clips.
    - morph weights are NOT normalised across targets, because morph targets are additive.

  AND IT IS WIRED (2026-09-21): `animate::apply` drives a scene's nodes through a `Skeleton`, which
  maps joints to nodes because a clip talks about joints and a scene is made of nodes. A channel the
  clip does not drive is LEFT ALONE, and a joint the skeleton does not map is REFUSED rather than
  skipped - animating part of a character and leaving the rest in its bind pose reads as a broken
  rig rather than as a mismatched pair.

  DONE (2026-09-21). The clip data model, every interpolation the item names, loop, clamp and
  ping-pong, cross-fade and blending between several clips, skeleton pose evaluation and the stated
  root-motion policy are all in `scene3d`'s `animate` and `deform`, and `conformance3d::extended`
  ranges over them - which is what the note here used to say was missing. An animation player has no
  emit path to wait on: what it produces is a POSE, and the pose is what skinning consumes.

- [x] A sorted transparent queue with stable back-to-front sorting. Weighted-blended OIT is an
  extension beyond this profile.
  DONE - AND IT WAS DONE BY THE CORE, which is why this closes with no code in it (checked
  2026-09-21 rather than assumed).

  THE FROZEN DOCUMENTS SETTLE WHOSE IT IS. `SCENE3D_PROFILE_1.md` - the CORE one - specifies the
  whole of it: the `Transparent` queue draws BACK TO FRONT by distance, the sort is STABLE so two
  drawables at one distance keep their submission order, the key is the distance to the bounding
  sphere's CENTRE rather than its nearest point, transparent depth is tested and not written, and a
  scene may not reorder the three queues. `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_1.md` says nothing about transparency
  sorting at all.

  AND `queue.rs` IMPLEMENTS IT WITH THE ONE SUBTLETY THAT MATTERS SAID OUT LOUD: `sort_far_to_near`
  reverses the DISTANCE and not the TIEBREAK, because reversing both would make two objects at one
  distance swap between the opaque and transparent queues.
  `objects_at_one_distance_keep_a_stable_order_in_both_directions` holds exactly that, and it is a
  core fixture that has been passing since before this part was activated.

  SO THIS LINE WAS A REQUIREMENT LISTED IN TWO PLACES, which the core/Extended split resolved in the
  core's favour and this file had not caught up with. What Extended would add is weighted-blended
  OIT, and the line itself says that is beyond the profile.

- [x] HDR and postprocessing: a linear HDR render target, tone mapping (the same operator
  `P02M0103a` fixes), the output transfer conversion, full-screen postprocess passes and fog, with
  bloom as a built-in effect and its threshold and kernel named. The general pass and shader model
  means further effects need no architectural change.

  DONE 2026-09-22. The operators, the `RGBA16F` target, the six-level pyramid's descriptions, the
  pass graph that orders the frame, fog, and the tone map - which is now literally the same operator
  as the 2D path's, with the disagreement between them closed rather than recorded.

  AND VERIFIED WHERE A CHANGE TO AN ENCODE PATH HAS TO BE, because this one is under every pixel
  either renderer produces: `host-tests` across 107 suites, `graphics-profile`, and the two gates
  that read frames off a real screen - `qemu-2d-demo` in 197 s and `qemu-3d-demo` in 145 s. Then the
  conformance suite on the target with both demos beside it, on all three ports: x86_64 4 passed in
  34 s, aarch64 4 passed in 170 s, riscv64 4 passed in 180 s.

  THE OPERATORS ARE IMPLEMENTED (2026-09-21) in `scene3d`'s `postprocess` module: the soft-knee
  bloom threshold, the 13-tap downsample and 9-tap tent upsample, exponential-squared fog, the tone
  map, and `resolve`, which is the profile's order - tone mapping LAST - in one place. The white
  point is read from the image-colour profile rather than written again, so there is one of it.

  AND TONE MAPPING IS WHY `pbr::shade` DOES NOT CLAMP ITS OUTPUT. Both bloom and fog are defined on
  linear radiance, so a material that saturated at 1 would have thrown the highlights away before
  bloom ever saw them. The fixture holds the consequence with both orders computed by hand: the
  profile's gives 1.546875 and mapping first gives 1.427938, which is a different picture rather
  than a rounding difference.

  A DEFECT IN THE 2D PATH WAS FOUND WHILE WRITING THIS AND IS NOT FIXED HERE. `graphics-core`'s
  `tone_mapped` passes a luminance at or below 1 through unchanged and applies extended Reinhard
  above it, and the curve is 0.53125 at 1 rather than the identity - so the 2D path reads 1.000000
  at a luminance of 1.0 and 0.531280 at 1.0001, a 47 per cent step across a boundary that runs
  through the middle of every lit surface. Nothing pins that behaviour and the image-colour profile
  says nothing about a threshold. The 3D operator implements what the profile NAMES, over the whole
  range and continuous; the 2D path is left alone because changing it changes every image that path
  produces, which is a decision about output rather than a fix an implementer takes in passing. THE
  TWO NOW DISAGREE BELOW A LUMINANCE OF 1, and reconciling them is this item's remaining question.

  **AND THE TARGET AND THE PASSES ARE IN (2026-09-21).** `environment::hdr_target_desc` describes the
  `RGBA16F` target the scene is drawn into, `postprocess::bloom_pyramid_desc` describes the six
  levels of the pyramid, and `postprocess::chain` builds the frame's pass graph: the lighting pass,
  six downsamples, five upsamples and the resolve, ordered by what each READS rather than by the
  order they were added in. Two fixtures hold it, and the order is read back out of the graph.

  TWO CHAINS OF TARGETS AND NOT ONE, which the graph is what forced. The tent upsample reads the
  level below it AND the level it is adding into, so writing back into that level makes a pass that
  samples what it writes - undefined in the resource model and a CYCLE in the graph, which is
  exactly what the first version returned. The descending passes write `pyramid` and the ascending
  ones write `ascent`, every target written once per frame, and a fixture asserts both of those
  properties rather than the shape that produced them.

  A RENDER TOO SMALL FOR SIX LEVELS IS REFUSED RATHER THAN GIVEN A SHORTER PYRAMID. The profile
  fixes the pyramid at six, so the coarsest level is a sixty-fourth of each dimension and sixty-four
  texels is the smallest render that has one - and a bloom built from four levels has a different
  shape at the same weight, so a small window would bloom DIFFERENTLY rather than not at all. The
  demo's 64x48 kernel-test size is below it in one dimension, which is a real consequence and is
  written down here rather than discovered.

  THE OUTPUT TRANSFER CONVERSION IS NOT IN THIS CRATE AND MUST NOT BE. The encode is the image-colour
  profile's, it already exists once in `graphics-core::color::encode`, and it is applied where a
  frame reaches a surface. A second copy in the scene layer is the shape this tree spends fixtures
  preventing; what this layer produces is LINEAR radiance, which is what the resolve's output is.

  **AND THE TWO ARE RECONCILED (2026-09-22), WITHOUT AMENDING THE FROZEN PROFILE AND WITHOUT MOVING
  A SINGLE PIXEL THAT WAS ALREADY INSIDE THE RANGE.**

  WHAT THE PROFILE ACTUALLY FIXES IS THE OPERATOR, AND NOT WHEN IT RUNS. `Image Colour Profile 1`
  names extended Reinhard on luminance with white at four times diffuse, and says separately that
  "clamping happens at OUTPUT and nowhere else". It does not say which conversions invoke the curve.
  The code decided that for itself and decided it PER PIXEL - pass a colour at or below a luminance
  of one through, scale everything above by the curve - and that is the 47 per cent step.

  THE OPERATOR MAPS `[0, white]` ONTO `[0, 1]`, so running it puts diffuse white at 0.53 of the
  output range. That is correct for a destination with four times the headroom and turns white into
  grey on one without, which is why the guard was there at all. A destination that reported nothing
  about what it can show has nowhere to map a highlight INTO - so the curve runs where there is
  headroom to run into, over the whole range as a global operator must, and where there is none the
  values clamp, which is the sentence the profile already carries for that case.

  ONE OPERATOR, AND WHAT DIFFERS IS THE DESTINATION. `scene3d::postprocess` always runs it, because
  a scene's radiances are high dynamic range by construction; `graphics-core`'s encoder runs it when
  its destination reported its luminance. Neither carries a second curve and neither carries a
  guard, so there is no boundary left for a step to live on.

  THREE THINGS CANNOT ALL HOLD, WHICH IS WHY THIS LOOKED LIKE A PREFERENCE AND IS NOT ONE: white
  stays at the top of the range, a highlight above one keeps its gradations, and the output range
  ends at one. The first two need room the third does not have. A knee below one was built and
  measured before this was settled - it buys the highlight and costs the white, 255 becomes 243, and
  it moved bright colours across the whole 2D conformance suite. Changing every picture the 2D path
  produces to buy detail above white on displays this system does not yet have is the wrong trade,
  and it was backed out with the measurement kept.

  TWO FIXTURES WERE REWRITTEN AND BOTH SAY MORE THAN THEY DID. `graphics-core`'s round trip asserted
  that four times diffuse white comes back inside the range; it now reads BOTH cases - a destination
  that reported nothing clamps and keeps its white, and one that reported its luminance has no step
  at diffuse white and keeps a highlight's gradations. `soft2d`'s additive-light case asserted that a
  narrow target compresses rather than clips; it now reads that such a target clamps AND that a
  colour inside the range is returned where the application put it, which is the half a compositor
  actually depends on.

  WHAT IS LEFT IS THE ONE QUESTION THE PARAGRAPH ABOVE OPENED, AND IT IS A DECISION ABOUT 2D OUTPUT.
  `graphics-core`'s `tone_mapped` is guarded twice - the caller applies it only to a colour with a
  channel above 1, and the function itself returns unchanged for a luminance at or below 1 - so the
  2D curve reads 1.000000 at a luminance of 1.0 and 0.531280 just above it. The 3D operator
  implements what the profile NAMES, continuously, over the whole range. The frozen rule is
  `extended Reinhard, on luminance` at `WHITE = 4.0` and it states no threshold, so the deviation is
  the 2D path's; but removing the guards darkens every image that path produces (mid-grey 0.5 maps
  to 0.34), which is a change to output and not a fix taken in passing.

  THE TWO CANDIDATE RESOLUTIONS, so whoever decides is deciding between stated things rather than
  discovering them:

    - **2D applies the frozen operator to everything**, both guards removed. One operator, one
      curve, no discontinuity anywhere - and every linear-working-space image drawn with tone
      mapping enabled gets globally darker. This is what the frozen profile literally says.
    - **The 2D flag means "compress what is out of range"**, which is what its guards are reaching
      for and what a compositor targeting an SDR display wants. That needs a curve that IS the
      identity below 1 and continuous above it, which is a DIFFERENT operator and therefore an
      amendment to the image-colour profile naming it - the fourth amendment of this shape.

  RECOMMENDED: the second, because the first makes a user interface darker to satisfy a rule written
  for scene radiance, and the guards say plainly that nobody wanted that.

  AND THE ANSWER TURNED OUT TO BE NEITHER OF THOSE TWO (2026-09-22), which is recorded above: both
  of them change what the 2D path draws, and the question the profile actually leaves open is not
  WHICH curve but WHEN it runs. The paragraphs above this one are kept because the reasoning they
  contain is what led to that, and because a reader who wants the knee back will want to see what it
  was measured to cost.

- [x] `Scene3DLimits` gains skeleton joints, morph targets, animation tracks, shadow maps and
  transparent items, each with a stated refusal on overflow.
  DONE 2026-09-21, and it is SIX limits rather than the seven this line asks for.

  THE FREEZE IS THE AUTHORITY AND IT NAMES SIX. `SCENE3D_EXTENDED_1.md`'s guaranteed-minimum table
  carries `max_shadow_cascades`, `max_shadow_maps`, `max_skeleton_joints`, `max_morph_targets`,
  `max_animation_tracks` and `environment_prefilter_levels`, and `max_transparent_items` is not
  among them. A transparent item IS a drawable, which the CORE profile's `max_drawables` already
  bounds, so the seventh would be a second bound on one thing - and adding a limit to a profile
  whose document is frozen is precisely the ordering defect `s-3d-ext` was added to prevent. The
  line above was written before that freeze; the freeze settled it.

  ZERO IS WHAT "DOES NOT CLAIM EXTENDED" LOOKS LIKE. `PROFILE_MINIMUM` carries zero in all six, so
  every Extended operation is refused against it by the same `LimitExceeded` the core ones use and
  no scene can acquire half the part by accident. `EXTENDED_MINIMUM` is the same scene at the
  Extended floor, and `claims_extended` is true only when all six are set - which is this part's own
  "entirely or not at all", made checkable.

  THREE FIXTURES HOLD IT, on the same terms as the core limits: every limit the frozen profile names
  is one this layer enforces AND nothing is enforced the profile does not name; claiming Extended
  moves no core limit, checked against the core list by name; and the whole-or-nothing rule is
  checked one omission at a time rather than on one example, because a predicate that had dropped a
  term would pass the example.

- [x] Host tests: LOD thresholds, skinning and morph evaluation against hand-computed poses,
  shadow-map matrix derivation, transparent sorting stability, PBR against its stated equations, and
  that every Extended feature emits only `render3d` commands.

  DONE 2026-09-21, ALL SIX, and the order they arrived in is recorded below because two of them were
  ticked once before they were checked and the check is what found real work.

  FOUR OF THE SIX WERE DONE FIRST (2026-09-21), and each is a fixture with a HAND-COMPUTED expected
  value rather than a round trip agreeing with itself: the detail ladder's thresholds and its hysteresis,
  skinning and morph against poses worked out by hand, the transparent queue's stability under equal
  distances, and the PBR terms asserted SEPARATELY beside their sum so a failure says which of the
  four moved.

  THE FIFTH IS IN NOW (2026-09-21), AND IT NEEDED A PROFILE AMENDMENT FIRST. There was no
  shadow-map matrix derivation in `shadow.rs` at all - it carried the bias, the cascade split, the
  PCF kernel and the cube face, and none of those is the light's view-projection - and there could
  not be one, because a map with an unstated projection has no one right pixel and the frozen profile
  did not state it. `SHADOW_RULES` gained "the projection fit" and `ExtendedFeature` gained
  `ShadowProjectionFit`, which moved the specification hash from `9ddee659` to `835cb674`: the
  amendment made LOUDLY, as this part's first one was.

  WHAT THE RULE PICKS, AND WHY EACH: a DIRECTIONAL cascade is fitted to the BOUNDING SPHERE of its
  slice rather than the slice's box, because a box changes size as the camera turns and a sphere does
  not - so the map's texel footprint stops changing every frame and the shadow edge stops crawling. A
  SPOT is a perspective of TWICE its outer cone, because a cone's half-angle is measured from its
  axis and a field of view across the frustum; halving it once too often covers the middle of the
  cone and leaves a shadow that ends in mid-air. A POINT is six perspectives of ninety degrees
  exactly - wider spends texels on what the neighbouring face already has, narrower leaves a
  cross-shaped seam of unshadowed surface radiating from the light - and their up vectors are the
  3D profile's cube-face orientation rather than six more vectors here, because two statements of one
  orientation is one of them being wrong later.

  Three fixtures hold it with values worked out from the geometry, and the sixth clause below is what
  is left.

  THE SIXTH WAS TICKED ONCE BEFORE IT WAS CHECKED, AND IT IS IN NOW (2026-09-21). The fixture named
  `a_scene_that_uses_every_feature_emits_only_render3d_commands` ranges over the CORE features:
  materials, instancing, culling, lights and a camera, and no Extended feature appears in it at all.
  `a_scene_that_uses_every_extended_feature_emits_only_render3d_commands` is the other one: a scene
  with a level-of-detail ladder that stays fine, a second that coarsens, a third that vanishes, a
  skeleton driven by a clip, a bound recomputed after morphing and skinning, a physically based
  material out of the second table, all three queues and a shadow caster pass.

  AND WRITING IT FOUND THAT ONE OF THOSE FEATURES REACHED NOTHING. The geometry source in it makes a
  mesh identifier its own vertex buffer, so the recorded list says WHICH level was drawn - and the
  first run said the fine one, because the recorder drew `drawable.mesh` and the chosen level lived
  in a `ViewDetail` nothing read. That is the join recorded against the level-of-detail item above;
  the fixture is what turned it from a feature that looked wired into one that is. The same
  assertion now reads the coarse mesh in the lighting pass and the same coarse mesh in the shadow
  pass, because a caster at a different level than its own surface is a shadow whose silhouette does
  not match the thing casting it.

  WHAT THE FIXTURE DOES NOT CLAIM: the environment cube, the HDR target and the shadow map are
  DESCRIPTIONS and emit no command at all, which the fixture names rather than passes over - so the
  list of commands it checks is read as complete rather than as a list that forgot three features.

- [x] Its own conformance and demo coverage, in its own executables or its own clearly separated
  phases - it does NOT extend `h`'s or `i`'s mandatory scene lists, because those are core gates.

  DONE 2026-09-21: the conformance half as its own module and its own tally, the demo half as its own
  phase of the same program, and neither reachable from a core gate.

  **AND THE PART'S OWN PERFORMANCE ROWS ARE MEASURED (2026-09-22)**, which its completion clause asks
  for beside the scenes and the phase. `docs/PERF.md` carries them: the Extended phase at the same
  three sizes as the core rows, measured with the same program and the same harness, with the shadow
  pass counted SEPARATELY from the lighting pass it feeds.

      surface      frame        rate       shadow pass   lighting pass
      320x240       489.2 ms    2.0 fps      40.1 ms        375.7 ms
      640x480      1512.8 ms    0.6 fps      37.9 ms       1362.4 ms
      800x600      2257.1 ms    0.4 fps      37.6 ms       2081.0 ms

  THE SHADOW PASS IS FLAT AT ALL THREE SIZES and that is what it is: it draws through the light's own
  512x512 map, whose extent belongs to the light and not to the window. The Extended material costs
  **6.08 us a fragment against the core material's 3.83** at 640x480 - a factor of 1.59 in which the
  GGX terms, the normal-map sample with its re-orthogonalised tangent frame, and the shadow lookup
  with its projective divide are the three things.

  AND THESE ROWS ARE NOT MEASURED AGAINST THE MILESTONE'S FRAME FLOOR, deliberately: that floor is
  closed by `i` over the CORE scene, and the benchmark item above keeps shadows and postprocessing
  out of it on purpose so an optional part cannot become a core gate through the frame budget.

  THE POSTPROCESS ROW IS THE ONE THAT IS NOT THERE, and the reason is the HDR item's: no backend runs
  a full-screen pass over an HDR target yet, so a bloom measurement would be arithmetic called in a
  loop written for the benchmark rather than a pass the system executes.

  **AND ALL THREE PORTS RUN IT (2026-09-22).** The Extended demo phase, the core demo and the
  conformance suite on the target were run together on each, because they share a program and a
  crate: aarch64 3 passed in 141 s, riscv64 3 passed in 145 s, x86_64 3 passed in 23 s. The Extended
  conformance list reports **62 passed, 0 failed, 0 unsupported, 0 untested** on all three.

  AND THE SHADOW MAP HOLDS 18,512 OF 262,144 TEXELS ON EVERY ONE OF THEM - the same number, not a
  number within a tolerance. A silhouette rendered through the same projection by three different
  rasterisers agreeing to the texel is what the geometry half of this stack being bit-exact by rule
  looks like from the outside.

  AND THE CORE GATES WERE RUN AFTERWARDS, because the Extended phase shares a program with the core
  demo and a shared `Vertex`, `Source` and `Targets` with it. `./check.sh --gate qemu-3d-demo` reads
  four runs off a real screen and passes in 59 s: animation, a bounded central object, a repeating
  ground texture, a blended panel, the overlay in the same frame, two stated poses, two aspect
  ratios and a restored console. `--gate graphics-profile` and `--gate host-tests` pass too, and the
  core demo's own kernel test passes beside the conformance run on the target. The phase is beside
  the core scene and the core scene is unchanged, measured rather than assumed.

  THE CONFORMANCE HALF IS DONE AND SEPARATE (2026-09-21). `conformance3d::extended` is its own module
  and its own tally: `Report` carries `extended` BESIDE the core tallies rather than inside them,
  `complete()` is the core claim and `complete_with_extended()` the other one - so a layer that
  implements the core and not the Extended list still passes the core gate, which is what keeps an
  optional part optional. 62 cases, green on x86_64, aarch64 and riscv64 (2026-09-22).

  **AND THE DEMO HALF IS IN (2026-09-21), AS A PHASE OF THE SAME PROGRAM AND NOT AN ADDITION TO ITS
  SCENE.** `test3d-sw --extended` draws a physically based sphere with a tangent-space normal map and
  a cast shadow INSTEAD OF the core cube, panel and ground. Instead of, because the core demo is a
  core gate and `i` closes on what it draws: a phase that added to that scene would make every core
  check depend on an optional profile being implemented, which is the clause about not extending
  `h`'s or `i`'s mandatory lists read the other way round. `check-qemu-3d-demo.sh` never passes
  `--extended`, so the core gate sees the scene it has always seen.

  WHAT THE PHASE IS, AND WHY EACH PIECE IS THE PIECE IT IS:

    - A SPHERE, because a normal map and a shadow are both invisible on a flat face: a surface whose
      direction never changes shows one constant perturbation, and the shadow of a box is a box. Its
      TANGENTS come from the parametrisation - the derivative of the position with respect to the
      longitude - rather than being solved from the triangles, which is exact per vertex and is
      defined at the poles where an averaged tangent is not.
    - A NORMAL MAP generated from the analytic slopes of a cosine height field, declared with `Normal`
      semantics so the sampler never transfer-decodes it. That is the rule that costs nothing to get
      wrong and changes every pixel.
    - THE PROFILE'S OWN THREE TERMS, written into the shader IR: GGX for the distribution, Smith
      height-correlated for the visibility - which CARRIES the `1 / (4 NoL NoV)` denominator, so the
      specular is `D * V * F` - and Schlick for the Fresnel with its fifth power by multiplication. A
      demo that wrote a different Smith term from the one `scene3d`'s fixtures hold would look right
      and prove nothing.
    - A SHADOW PASS THAT WRITES DEPTH INTO A COLOUR ATTACHMENT, and this is the one place the profile
      forced the shape rather than the other way round: `Render3D Core Profile 1` has no depth-texture
      binding and no comparison sampler, so what the light wrote has to reach the lighting pass as
      ordinary texels. The frame reads the attachment back into a texture between the two passes. On
      a software renderer that is a copy of one small buffer; on a GPU it is where a real backend
      would bind the attachment directly.
    - THE SHADOW PASS CULLS FRONT FACES, which is the opposite of every other pass in the program. It
      records where the light STOPS on the far side of the caster, which puts the recorded depth
      behind the lit surface - so the self-shadowing error lands on the side facing away from the
      camera rather than across the middle of the object, and the bias only has to cover the
      terminator.
    - AND THE `v` AXIS IS FLIPPED IN THE LOOKUP, because clip space climbs upward and a texture's rows
      run downward. A map sampled without the flip shadows the mirror image of the scene, which looks
      like an offset and is chased in the bias for hours.

  THE LIGHT IS THE PHASE'S OWN AND POINTS THE OTHER WAY ACROSS `z`. A shadow falls away from its
  light, so a light behind the object puts the shadow behind it too - where the object hides it. This
  one stands in front and to the side. The ambient is lower than the core scene's for the same
  reason: ambient is what a shadowed surface is lit by, and a scene whose ambient equals its direct
  light has shadows nobody can find.

  AND IT IS PROVED BY A GOVERNED KERNEL TEST, on the same terms as the core demo's:
  `kernel.applications.the_3d_demo_draws_the_extended_scene_with_a_cast_shadow` runs the staged PIE
  at 64x48 against the stand-in surface host and reads THREE things that fail for three different
  reasons.

    - The demo reports how many of the light's map hold a caster. Zero is what a light pointed away
      from the scene writes, what a projection too small to contain the object writes, and what a
      pass that never ran leaves behind - and all three produce a picture with no shadow in it, which
      is also what a correct scene with nothing to cast one looks like. The run reads
      **18,512 of 262,144 texels**, which is the sphere's silhouette at this projection and this map.
    - The frame carries more than eight distinct colours, which is a rendered scene rather than a
      cleared surface.
    - And the GROUND is read out of the frame. It is one flat quad of one material under one
      directional light, so the only thing that can darken part of it is something standing between
      it and the light: the brightest of the bottom rows must exceed the darkest by half again, and
      the darkest must be on the side the light's direction puts it. A shadow on the wrong side is a
      sign flip in the projection, which a check that only looked for darkness would pass.

  GREEN ON x86_64 (2026-09-21), 1 s.

  THE ONE THING THE SHADOW MAP DOES NOT FOLLOW IS THE WINDOW. Its extent is the LIGHT's box and not
  the surface's, so it stays 512x512 whatever the scene renders at - which is why a 64x48 kernel test
  still exercises a map of a realistic size, and why a resize rebuilds every plan except this one.

## P02M0103g - soft3d, the complete CPU implementation of Profile 1

The software 3D backend. It implements the WHOLE of `Render3D Core Profile 1`; a profile feature it
cannot execute is a defect, not an `Unsupported`.

- [x] Split `soft3d` into PREPARE and EXECUTE the way `soft2d` is split, and for the same reason:
  resource upload, shader validation and lowering, pipeline creation, command-list validation and
  tile/bin scratch reservation all happen BEFORE submission, and `submit(prepared)` then allocates
  nothing in steady state. Its internal execute phase fulfills the `Submission` contract from `e`. Without that boundary, programmable shaders, pipeline creation, mipmap
  generation and the pass graph each become a place a frame can start allocating, and the
  no-steady-state-allocation requirement in this milestone becomes untestable on the 3D side while
  being enforced on the 2D one.
  DONE 2026-09-14. `soft3d::frame` is the boundary: `prepare` validates both shader modules against
  the device's limits, checks that the two stages agree about every varying LOCATION AND TYPE, checks
  that the pipeline's shading rate is the one its fragment stage's inputs force, refuses a draw
  naming a pipeline the plan does not hold or asking for zero instances, and sizes the tile and bin
  storage. `execute` then walks a plan that has already been checked.
  A FIXTURE ASSERTS THE STEADY STATE RATHER THAN ASSUMING IT: three identical frames, with the
  scratch capacity read between them, and the assertion that it did not grow after the first - which
  is what makes "allocates nothing in steady state" a property of the boundary rather than of the
  code happening not to allocate today.
  AND `execute` IS THE SOFTWARE BACKEND'S `Submission`: it runs to completion and reports, which is
  the already-completed-ticket shape the submission model was written to admit beside a GPU
  backend's pending one.

- [x] Implement the rasteriser with these conventions fixed: sample points at pixel centres
  `(x + 0.5, y + 0.5)` at 1x, and the frozen profile positions at 2x/4x; fixed-point edge
  equations rather than floating-point comparisons, so
  coverage is exact and shared edges cannot double-fill or leave a seam; the top-left fill rule;
  deterministic back-face culling by winding; perspective-correct interpolation. Degenerate and
  off-screen primitives are skipped deterministically; non-finite positions are typed refusals
  under the clipper/IR contract. A SUB-PIXEL triangle is NOT
  skipped: it either covers a sample point or it does not, and dropping it by size makes small
  geometry flicker as the camera moves.

  The numbers, because "fixed-point" without them is half a contract and identical coverage on three
  architectures is what the other half decides: `SUBPIXEL_BITS = 8`, `MAX_RASTER_EXTENT = 16_384`,
  float viewport coordinates quantised by round-half-away-from-zero, edge equations in checked `i64`.
  Those constants make the arithmetic checkable: clipped viewport coordinates lie in `0..=2^22`
  subpixel units, so coordinate differences are at most `2^22` in magnitude, each edge product is
  at most `2^44`, and subtracting two products is bounded by `2^45`, within `i64`. Validate that
  coordinate bound before edge evaluation and record the proof beside the constants; a coordinate
  equal to `2^22` does not fit a signed 22-bit representation. All of this is on the BIT-EXACT side of the
  split at the top of this file.
  DONE 2026-09-14. `src/user/libs/graphics/soft3d` is the crate; `fixed` holds the grid and `raster`
  the setup, binning and coverage.
  THE PROOF IS WRITTEN OUT BESIDE THE CONSTANTS AND A FIXTURE IS THE ARITHMETIC IT IS ABOUT: two
  coordinates at the extremes of the legal range produce an edge value the fixture holds against
  `2^45`, and against `2^43` from below - because a bound the worst case never reaches is a bound
  about nothing. A coordinate EQUAL to `2^22` is admitted and one past it refused, which is the
  off-by-one a check written as "fits in twenty-two bits" gets wrong.
  A BACK-FACING TRIANGLE IS REWOUND RATHER THAN GIVEN ITS OWN PATH, so the edge tests, the fill rule
  and the barycentric weights are one piece of code instead of two with opposite comparisons - and
  two comparisons that must be exact mirror images is how a front face and a back face that share an
  edge get a seam. The original facing is carried for the fragment stage, and a fixture holds it
  against `render-math`'s `facing` on the NDC positions BEFORE the viewport inverts Y, which is the
  one place the convention could be backwards.
  THE TOP-LEFT RULE HAS THE FIXTURE IT EXISTS FOR: a quad split along its diagonal, every sample
  counted, and the assertion that no sample is filled twice and none is missed - which is what a
  floating-point edge test cannot guarantee and this fixed-point one can. A sub-pixel triangle is
  kept and answers by coverage; a degenerate one produces nothing on every architecture; a coordinate
  that was never clipped is a refusal rather than a clamp.

- [x] Implement qualifier-aware clipping of position and every varying. For edge endpoints `P0` and
  `P1`, the homogeneous intersection parameter `t` gives `P = (1-t)*P0 + t*P1`. Position and `smooth`
  varyings use that parameter. A `noperspective` varying uses the projected parameter
  `u = t*w1 / ((1-t)*w0 + t*w1)` and value `(1-u)*a0 + u*a1`, so it remains linear after perspective
  division. Use the profile's canonical clip coordinates and intersection/rounding rules; the
  existing `w == 0` refusal covers a non-projectable intersection rather than substituting `t`.
  These varying calculations remain on the tolerance-based side of the geometry/shading split.

  For `flat`, capture the original assembled primitive's provoking-vertex value before clipping and
  copy it unchanged to every resulting primitive. Never interpolate it or choose a new provoking
  value when clipping removes the original vertex or when the clipped polygon is fan-triangulated.
  The frozen per-topology provoking rule applies to triangles, lines and points alike. Repeated
  clipping preserves the same field and original flat value through every plane; normal, UV and
  colour semantics do not override the shader's declared interpolation qualifier.

  The six planes for the chosen right-handed `[0, 1]` clip space, each as the inequality a vertex
  satisfies to be inside:

      left    x + w >= 0        bottom  y + w >= 0        near    z     >= 0
      right   w - x >= 0        top     w - y >= 0        far     w - z >= 0

  With the degenerate cases decided: a vertex with `w == 0` is not projectable and its primitive is
  REFUSED; a zero intersection denominator produces no new vertex and the result follows from the
  inside/outside classification; a non-finite coordinate fails the primitive with `NonFinite`. The
  bound is a number, not a promise of one: Sutherland-Hodgman against a convex volume adds at most
  one vertex per half-space, so `MAX_CLIPPED_VERTICES = 3 + 6 = 9` and
  `MAX_TRIANGLES_AFTER_CLIP = 9 - 2 = 7` under fan triangulation. Those live in the clipper and are
  proven, not configured.
  DONE 2026-09-14. `soft3d::clip` cuts the three qualifiers by their three rules and a fixture
  separates them: on a foreshortened edge - one endpoint at `w = 1` and the other at `w = 9` - the
  homogeneous parameter and the projected one give values that differ by more than a twentieth, which
  is the kink at the clip edge that using one rule for both produces.
  A `flat` ATTRIBUTE IS CAPTURED BEFORE THE CUT AND SURVIVES THE REMOVAL OF ITS OWN VERTEX, which a
  fixture shows by putting the provoking vertex far outside the volume and requiring the identity to
  come back unchanged. Nothing recomputes it, because by the time a vertex has been cut away there
  is nothing left to read.
  The classification is `render3d`'s own, so this clipper and the API's agree about what is outside:
  a non-finite component refuses the primitive, everything behind the eye culls, and a zero
  intersection denominator produces no new vertex rather than an infinity whose sign decides the
  shape. The bound is an ASSERTION and not a clamp - exceeding it means the algorithm is not the one
  the proof is about - and a deterministic sweep of two dozen corner-cutting triangles holds every
  one of them to nine vertices and seven triangles.

- [x] Implement the shader interpreter for `render-shader` IR - both stages, all interpolation
  qualifiers, typed uniform and resource binding, bounded loops and checked access - with a
  deterministic scalar path. Position dependencies use the StrictF32/ClipCoordQ contract above;
  relaxed shading results are on the tolerance side. The interpreter cannot classify geometry from
  a position evaluated under relaxed transcendental semantics.
  DONE 2026-09-14. `soft3d::interpreter` runs both stages over `soft3d::value::Val`, which is one
  representation - a type plus a sequence of 32-bit words - for every IR type. The alternative, an
  enumeration with a variant per shape, makes every operation a nested match over two operand shapes,
  which is where an interpreter silently does the wrong thing for one combination.
  SCALAR AND NOT VECTORISED, AND THAT IS THE CONTRACT. This is the reference every faster path is
  differential-tested against; a reference that was itself vectorised would have nothing to be tested
  against, and the first time a SIMD path disagreed there would be no way to say which was right.
  THE DEGENERATE CASES HAVE THEIR OWN FIXTURES, each with the reason it matters: a zero-length vector
  normalises to itself and not to a NaN, because one NaN in a lighting term makes a whole surface
  black; `fract(-0.25)` is `0.75` and a modulo is EUCLIDEAN, so a repeating coordinate does not depend
  on the sign convention of the host language; an integer division by zero answers zero rather than
  trapping, because a shader has no way to report one and a trap would take the frame down; a
  comparison against a NaN is false except for `NotEqual`, which is IEEE's rule and the one a
  different answer would turn into a different branch and a different triangle.
  A DISCARD ENDS THE STAGE rather than setting a flag and running on: continuing would execute stores
  whose results are thrown away, and a texture read among them is work a discarded fragment paid for.
  A computed index outside its array is refused AT THE ACCESS, which is the case validation cannot
  decide because the value is not known until it is computed.
  AND THE POSITION CONTRACT IS NOT RE-DECIDED HERE. `render-shader::validate` refuses a position that
  depends on an operation the profile does not bound at zero ULP, so the interpreter does not need to
  know which transcendental is which - and must not try, because a second rule here could disagree
  with the first.

- [x] Implement every pass and attachment feature in the profile: multiple colour attachments,
  load/clear/discard and store/discard per attachment, offscreen targets and render-to-texture, all
  five depth/stencil formats with the full compare, write, mask and stencil-operation set, depth
  bias, and MSAA at 1x/2x/4x with sample masks, alpha-to-coverage and resolve.
  DONE 2026-09-14. `soft3d::pass` owns the attachments and the fragment path; `soft3d::frame` drives
  them. Every colour attachment carries its OWN blend state and write mask, because a pass that
  writes colour to one and object identities to another must blend the first and not the second -
  and an attachment the shader did not write is LEFT ALONE rather than cleared, since a pass that
  erased what it did not touch could not be built up.
  THE DEPTH AND STENCIL TEST RUNS ONCE PER FRAGMENT, not once per attachment: running it per
  attachment would test the same fragment several times and write the depth buffer more than once
  for one fragment. The order within it is the frozen one - sample mask, alpha-to-coverage, stencil,
  depth, blend, write mask - and a fixture shows the stencil's `on_depth_fail` operation running
  rather than its `on_fail`, which is what an implementation that tested depth first would get
  backwards for most of the fragments a stencil is used for.
  A DISCARDED ATTACHMENT IS POISONED AT BOTH ENDS rather than left. `Discard` says nobody may read
  it, and a frame that reads what it discarded then looks wrong EVERYWHERE instead of looking right
  on the machine it was developed on. Render-to-texture RESOLVES first and declares the result linear
  and premultiplied, because a texture that claimed otherwise would be decoded a second time on its
  first fetch - the double-decode that darkens a render-to-texture chain at every step - and an
  identity attachment comes back with `Identity` semantics, never filtered and never converted.
  The depth bias is the frozen equation, applied AFTER the depth is computed and BEFORE the test and
  the write, with the slope taken from the triangle's own plane.

- [x] Implement the full sampler and texture set: 2D, cube, array and 3D textures; mipmap generation
  and selection; nearest and linear min/mag and mip filtering; trilinear; clamp, repeat, mirror and
  border addressing; LOD bias and min/max LOD; depth-compare sampling for shadow maps; and
  anisotropic filtering to `max_anisotropy` 8 by a stated number of taps along the major axis, with
  the LOD and footprint derived from the shader derivatives. Cube-map face selection and orientation,
  seam handling across faces, border colours, the depth-compare return value, 3D filtering, and the
  filter used when generating mipmaps - including that generation happens in LINEAR light and that a
  `Data` or `NormalMap` image is never transfer-decoded on the way - are all stated. Texel centres are at
  `(i + 0.5) / extent`, and `repeat` is defined for NEGATIVE and large coordinates by Euclidean
  remainder so `-0.25` and `0.75` sample the same texel rather than differing by the sign convention
  of the language's `%`. sRGB texels are DECODED to linear before filtering and premultiplied before
  interpolation; blending is evaluated in LINEAR LIGHT.
  DONE 2026-09-14. `soft3d::texture` implements every rule the profile freezes, each with a fixture
  holding it against a value worked out from the definition.
  THE ONES WORTH NAMING, BECAUSE EACH IS A PLACE TWO BACKENDS DISAGREE SILENTLY. A filtered sRGB
  sample averages in LINEAR light - encoded `0.5` and `1.0` give `0.607` and not the encoded average
  of `0.75` - while a `Data` or `Normal` image goes through no transfer at all, because a roughness
  value and a tangent-space direction are numbers rather than light. A filtered sample PREMULTIPLIES
  first, so a transparent white texel does not bleed its colour into its neighbour, which is the halo
  around every cut-out leaf ever rendered. A DEPTH-COMPARE sample compares each tap FIRST and filters
  the results, so a linear one is the FRACTION of taps that passed - filtering the depths and
  comparing once gives a hard edge, which is what makes a shadow map look like a stencil.
  The level comes from the MAXIMUM of the two derivative lengths and not their geometric mean, and a
  fixture shows the two differ by a whole level on a stretched footprint. Anisotropy takes its level
  from the MINOR axis and its taps along the major one; a fixture shows the anisotropic sample keeping
  a stripe the isotropic one has averaged away, and that `max_anisotropy` of one collapses to exactly
  the isotropic answer.
  A cube's taps at a face edge are RE-PROJECTED through their own directions, so one that leaves the
  face lands on the neighbour rather than on a clamped edge texel - a construction that needs no table
  of edge adjacencies and so cannot get one of them backwards. An ARRAY layer is addressed by index
  and never filtered between, which is the whole difference between an array and a 3D texture and the
  one a sampler written for the wrong one gets backwards; a layer the texture does not have is
  refused rather than clamped to the last, which would draw the wrong page of an atlas.
  Mips are a 2x2 box in the texture's OWN numeric space - linear light for a colour texture, because
  a box over encoded values makes every level darker than the one above it - and an odd dimension
  halves by `max(1, floor(n/2))` with the box taking the texels that exist, since there is no
  weighting to invent.

  **AND A DEFECT IN THAT LAST RULE WAS FOUND AND FIXED (2026-09-19): THE TOP LEVEL WAS NOT DECODED.**
  Generating a chain decodes the top level, filters every level below it from that decoded light, and
  then DECLARES the texture linear and premultiplied so the next fetch does not decode it a second
  time. The declaration was right and the top level was not replaced: the line meant to store the
  decoded form assigned level zero to itself, and the decoded copy that every level below it was
  built from went out of scope with the loop. So an sRGB texture came out of `generate_mips` with
  level zero still ENCODED and a texture that said it was not.
  WHAT THAT LOOKS LIKE IS WHY IT SURVIVED. Nothing is missing and nothing crashes: a MAGNIFIED
  fragment reads level zero and gets the encoded number treated as light - on sRGB `0.5` reads as
  `0.5` where the chain's own answer is `0.214`, more than twice - and every minified fragment is
  correct, so the texture has a step between level zero and level one that no filter put there and
  that only appears at the distance where the two meet. A straight-alpha source was not premultiplied
  either, which is the same bug's other half.
  THE TEST NEXT TO IT CHECKED THE CHAIN AND NOT THE TOP. It asserted the declared transfer and that
  level ONE averaged decoded values, which is exactly the assertion the defect passes. It now pins
  level zero as well, and a second case pins that a straight-alpha source comes out premultiplied at
  every level; restoring the self-assignment fails the first on `0.5` against `0.214`.

- [x] Implement the geometry set: all six topologies - triangle list, strip and fan, line list and
  strip, point list - with primitive restart, u16 and u32 indices, multiple vertex streams,
  configurable attributes, and instancing with base vertex and base instance. Multiple vertex streams
  and configurable attributes are what a skinned or morphed mesh later needs, and naming them that way
  is the whole of `g`'s relationship to `f-ext`: this item owns the PRIMITIVES, not the features
  built on them, and `g` is done without either (clarified 2026-08-30).
  DONE 2026-09-14, AND THE PROFILE GAINED THE RULES IT WAS MISSING. A profile that mandates six
  topologies and specifies one is a profile with five gaps, so `graphics-profile::render3d_spec` now
  freezes ten LINE AND POINT rules beside the triangle ones: line coverage by the DIAMOND-EXIT rule
  and why it rather than a midpoint walk, the line width, the interpolation parameter along a line,
  point size and its clamp, point coverage as a half-open square, that BACK-FACE CULLING APPLIES TO
  TRIANGLES ONLY, how lines and points clip, and their depth rule.
  THE DIAMOND-EXIT RULE IS THE LINE'S VERSION OF THE TOP-LEFT RULE and has the same fixture: two
  segments meeting end to end light the shared pixel EXACTLY once - the first ends inside its diamond
  and does not light it, the second starts there and leaves. A horizontal line exactly on a row
  boundary is TANGENT to every diamond and lights nothing, which is correct and which a fixture
  asserts rather than hides. A point centred on a pixel corner lands in the pixel below and left of
  it, the same half-open tie-break a triangle's edges make, which is what lets two adjacent points
  tile without overlapping.
  `soft3d::geometry` assembles all six topologies with the winding of a strip ALTERNATING so every
  triangle of it faces the same way; primitive restart ends a strip or a fan and is REFUSED on a
  list, which has nothing to restart; the base vertex is signed and the SUM is checked, because a
  negative base with a small index is a read before the buffer that a per-draw check cannot catch.
  Instancing is the frame driver's: the vertex stage runs once per instance with the instance index
  reaching it, and a fixture draws three instances of one triangle into three separate places.

  The clipping and raster rules above are written for TRIANGLES, and the other two kinds need their
